# Jews

"Jew" redirects here. For the word, see [*Jew* (word)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jew_\(word\)). For other uses, see [Jew (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jew_\(disambiguation\)).

This article is about the ethnoreligious group. For the religion itself, see [Judaism](15624.md).

**Jews**,[^cite_note-17] or the **Jewish people**, are an [ethnoreligious group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnoreligious_group)[^cite_note-Jews-are-ethnoreligious-group-18] and [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation),[^cite_note-Nicholson20022-19][^cite_note-20] originating in the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) of [ancient Israel and Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ancient_Israel_and_Judah).[^cite_note-21] They traditionally adhere to [Judaism](15624.md).[^cite_note-Britannica-22][^cite_note-23] Jewish ethnicity, religion, and community are highly interrelated,[^cite_note-Lederhendler20012-24][^cite_note-Yee20052-25] as Judaism is an [ethnic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_religion),[^cite_note-Nicholson20022-19][^cite_note-Dowty19982-26] though many ethnic Jews do not practice it.[^cite_note-KrauszTulea2-27][^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] Jews regard [converts to Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerim) as members of the Jewish nation, pursuant to the [long-standing conversion process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism).[^cite_note-KrauszTulea2-27][^cite_note-30]

The Israelites emerged from the pre-existing [Canaanite peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_peoples) to establish the kingdoms of [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)) and [Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah) in the [Southern Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Levant) during the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age).[^cite_note-Day-31] Originally, Jews referred to the inhabitants of Judah,[^cite_note-Jastrow-2005-32] and were distinguished from the [gentiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentile) and the [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans).[^cite_note-Amy-2006-33] According to the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible), these inhabitants predominantly originate from the [tribe of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_of_Judah), who were descendants of [Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_\(Bible\)), the fourth son of [Jacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob).[^cite_note-Jew-34] The [tribe of Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_of_Benjamin) were another significant demographic in Judah and were considered Jews too.[^cite_note-Encyclopedia_Britannica-35] By the late 6th century BCE, Judaism had evolved from the Israelite religion, dubbed [Yahwism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahwism) by modern scholars,[^cite_note-MINDELL2009-36] having a theology that religious Jews believe to be the expression of the [Mosaic covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic_covenant) between [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism) and the Jewish people.[^cite_note-Knowledge_Resources:_Judaism-37] After the [Babylonian exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_exile), Jews referred to followers of Judaism,[^cite_note-Encyclopedia_Britannica-35][^cite_note-Lau-2009-38] descendants of the Israelites,[^cite_note-Thiessen-2011a-39][^cite_note-Goodman-2006-40][^cite_note-41] citizens of [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea),[^cite_note-Penner-42][^cite_note-Schwartz-2014-43] or allies of the Judean state.[^cite_note-Cohen-2001-6][^cite_note-Schwartz-2021-44][^cite_note-Yarden-2022-45] Jewish migration within the Mediterranean region during the [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period), followed by [population transfers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer), caused by events like the [Jewish–Roman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_wars), gave rise to the [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora), consisting of diverse Jewish communities that maintained their sense of [Jewish history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_history), [identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity), and [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture).[^cite_note-46]

In the following millennia, Jewish diaspora communities [coalesced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalescent_theory) into three major [ethnic subdivisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions) according to where their ancestors settled: the *[Ashkenazim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews)* ([Central](5188.md) and [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe)), the *[Sephardim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews)* ([Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula), later [North Africa](21714.md)), and the *[Mizrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews)* ([Asia](689.md) and sometimes includes [North Africa](21714.md)).[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-Dosick-48] While these three major divisions account for most of the world's Jews, there are other smaller Jewish groups outside of the three.[^cite_note-49] Prior to [World War II](32927.md), the [global Jewish population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population_by_country) reached a peak of 16.7 million,[^cite_note-JVIL2010-50] representing around 0.7% of the world's population at that time. During World War II, approximately six million Jews throughout [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Europe) were systematically murdered by [Nazi Germany](21212.md) in a genocide known as [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] Since then, the population has slowly risen again, and as of 2021, was estimated to be at 15.2 million by the demographer [Sergio Della Pergola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Della_Pergola)[^cite_note-JDB-3] or less than 0.2% of the total world population in 2012.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-55] Today, over 85% of Jews live in [Israel](9282173.md) or the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_United_States). Israel, whose population is 73.9% Jewish, is the only country where Jews comprise more than 2.5% of the population.[^cite_note-JDB-3]

Jews have significantly influenced and contributed to the development and growth of human progress in many fields, both historically and in modern times, including in [science and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture#Science_and_technology),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_philosophy),[^cite_note-57] [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethics),[^cite_note-Sekine-2005-58] [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_literature),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] [governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_political_movements),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] [business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture#Economic_activity),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_art), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_music), [comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_humor), [theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_theatre),[^cite_note-59] [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture#Cinema), [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Israel),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] [food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_cuisine), [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_medicine),[^cite_note-Rabin-60][^cite_note-Shatzmiller,_Joseph_1995-61] and [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements). Jews founded [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Christianity)[^cite_note-BarclaySweet1996-62] and had [an indirect but profound influence on Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic%E2%80%93Jewish_relations).[^cite_note-Paterson2009-63] In these ways and others, Jewish culture has played a significant role in the development of [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture).[^cite_note-Cambridge_University_Historical_Series-64][^cite_note-britannica.com-65]

## Name and etymology

Main article: [Jew (word)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jew_\(word\))

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of Jewish ethnonyms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_ethnonyms).

The term "Jew" is derived from the [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) word יְהוּדִי *Yehudi*, with the [plural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plural) יְהוּדִים *Yehudim*.[^cite_note-EJ253-66] [Endonyms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endonym) in other [Jewish languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_language) include the [Ladino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladino_language) ג׳ודיו *Djudio* (plural ג׳ודיוס, *Djudios*) and the [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_language) ייִד *Yid* (plural ייִדן *Yidn*).

The [Book of Genesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Genesis) 29\:35 and 49\:8 connect "Judah" with the verb *yada*, meaning "praise". Some scholars state that "Judah" derives from the name of a [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) geographic region dominated by gorges and ravines.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] The gradual [ethnonymic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) shift from "[Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites)" to "Jews", irrespective of whether their descent was from [Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_\(son_of_Jacob\)) or [Jacob (Israel)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob), although not mentioned in the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah), does appear later in the [Hebrew Bible (Tanakh)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible), in the [Book of Esther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Esther) (4th century BCE).[^cite_note-69] Some modern scholars disagree with the conflation, based on the works of [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus), [Philo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philo) and [Apostle Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Paul).[^cite_note-70]

The English word "Jew" is a derivation of [Middle English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_English) *_Gyw, Iewe_*. The latter was loaned from the [Old French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_French) *_giu_*, which itself evolved from the earlier *_juieu_*, which in turn derived from *_judieu/iudieu_* which through [elision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elision) had dropped the letter "d" from the [Medieval Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin) *Iudaeus*, which, like the New Testament [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek) term *[Ioudaios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioudaios)*, meant both "Jew" and "[Judean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judean)" / "of [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea)".[^cite_note-71] The Greek term was a loan from [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Aramaic) *_\*yahūdāy_*, corresponding to Hebrew יְהוּדִי *Yehudi*.[^cite_note-Jew-34]

Some scholars prefer translating *Ioudaios* as "Judean" in the Bible since it is more precise, denotes the community's origins and prevents readers from engaging in antisemitic [eisegesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eisegesis).[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] Others disagree, believing that it erases the Jewish identity of Biblical characters such as [Jesus](1095706.md).[^cite_note-Amy-2006-33] Daniel R. Schwartz distinguishes "Judean" and "Jew". Here, "Judean" refers to the inhabitants of Judea, which encompassed southern [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)). Meanwhile, "Jew" refers to the descendants of Israelites that adhere to [Judaism](15624.md). Converts are included in the definition.[^cite_note-Schwartz-2014-43] But [Shaye J. D. Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaye_J._D._Cohen) argues that "Judean" is inclusive of believers of the Judean God and allies of the Judean state.[^cite_note-Cohen-2001-6] Another scholar, [Jodi Magness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jodi_Magness), wrote the term *Ioudaioi* refers to a "people of Judahite/Judean ancestry who worshipped the God of Israel as their national deity and (at least nominally) lived according to his laws."[^cite_note-74]

The etymological equivalent is in use in other languages, e.g., يَهُودِيّ *yahūdī* (sg.), *al-yahūd* (pl.), in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic), "Jude" in [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language), "judeu" in [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language), "Juif" (m.)/"Juive" (f.) in [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language), "jøde" in [Danish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_language) and [Norwegian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_language), "judío/a" in [Spanish](26825.md), "jood" in [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language), "żyd" in [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) etc., but derivations of the word "Hebrew" are also in use to describe a Jew, e.g., in [Italian](14708.md) (*Ebreo*), in [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language) ("Ebri/Ebrani" ([Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language): عبری/عبرانی)) and [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) (*Еврей, Yevrey*).[^cite_note-75] The German word "Jude" is pronounced [\[ˈjuːdə\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Standard_German), the corresponding [adjective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjective) "jüdisch" [\[ˈjyːdɪʃ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Standard_German) (Jewish) is the origin of the word "Yiddish".[^cite_note-76]

According to *[The American Heritage Dictionary of the English Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_American_Heritage_Dictionary_of_the_English_Language)*, fourth edition (2000),

> It is widely recognized that the attributive use of the noun *Jew*, in phrases such as *Jew lawyer* or *Jew ethics*, is both vulgar and highly offensive. In such contexts *Jewish* is the only acceptable possibility. Some people, however, have become so wary of this construction that they have extended the stigma to any use of *Jew* as a noun, a practice that carries risks of its own. In a sentence such as *There are now several Jews on the council*, which is unobjectionable, the substitution of a circumlocution like *Jewish people* or *persons of Jewish background* may in itself cause offense for seeming to imply that *Jew* has a negative connotation when used as a noun.[^cite_note-77]

## Identity

Main articles: [Who is a Jew?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_is_a_Jew%3F), [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity), and [Jewish peoplehood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_peoplehood)



17th century map of [Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan)

[Judaism](15624.md) shares some of the characteristics of a [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation),[^cite_note-Nicholson2002-78][^cite_note-Neusner1991-79][^cite_note-Dowty1998-80][^cite_note-Brandeis-1915-81][^cite_note-Palmer-2002-82][^cite_note-83] an [ethnicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity),[^cite_note-Jews-are-ethnoreligious-group-18] a [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion), and a [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture),[^cite_note-GordisHeller2012-84][^cite_note-Kunin2000-85][^cite_note-Mendes-Flohr1991-86] making the definition of [who is a Jew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_is_a_Jew) vary slightly depending on whether a religious or national approach to identity is used.[^cite_note-87] Generally, in modern secular usage, Jews include three groups: people who were born to a Jewish family regardless of whether or not they follow the religion, those who have some Jewish ancestral background or lineage (sometimes including those who do not have strictly [matrilineal descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrilineality_in_Judaism)), and people without any Jewish ancestral background or lineage who have formally [converted to Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism) and therefore are followers of the religion.[^cite_note-88] In the context of biblical and classical literature, Jews could refer to inhabitants of the Kingdom of Judah,[^cite_note-Jastrow-2005-32][^cite_note-Amy-2006-33][^cite_note-Jew-34][^cite_note-Encyclopedia_Britannica-35] or the broader [Judean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea) region,[^cite_note-Penner-42][^cite_note-Schwartz-2014-43][^cite_note-89] allies of the Judean state,[^cite_note-Cohen-2001-6][^cite_note-Schwartz-2021-44][^cite_note-Yarden-2022-45] or anyone that followed Judaism.[^cite_note-Martin-2003-90][^cite_note-Lau-2009-38]

Historical definitions of [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity) have traditionally been based on *[halakhic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha)* definitions of matrilineal descent, and halakhic conversions. These definitions of who is a Jew date back to the codification of the [Oral Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_Torah) into the [Babylonian Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud), around 200 [CE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era). Interpretations by Jewish sages of sections of the Tanakh – such as [Deuteronomy 7\:1–5](https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Deuteronomy%207\:1%E2%80%935\&version=nrsv), which forbade intermarriage between their [Israelite ancestors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) and seven non-Israelite nations: "for that \[i.e. giving your daughters to their sons or taking their daughters for your sons,] would turn away your children from following me, to serve other gods"[^cite_note-Day-31] – are used as a warning against [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage_in_Judaism) between Jews and gentiles. [Leviticus 24\:10](https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Leviticus%2024\:10\&version=nrsv) says that the son in a marriage between a Hebrew woman and an Egyptian man is "of the community of Israel." This is complemented by [Ezra 10\:2–3](https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Ezra%2010\:2%E2%80%933\&version=nrsv), where Israelites returning from Babylon vow to put aside their [gentile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentile) wives and their children.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] A popular theory is that the rape of Jewish women in captivity brought about the law of Jewish identity being inherited through the maternal line, although scholars challenge this theory citing the Talmudic establishment of the law from the pre-exile period.[^cite_note-Klein2016-93] Another argument is that the rabbis changed the law of patrilineal descent to matrilineal descent due to the widespread rape of Jewish women by Roman soldiers.[^cite_note-Schott2010-94] Since the anti-religious *[Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah)* movement of the late 18th and 19th centuries, *halakhic* interpretations of Jewish identity have been challenged.[^cite_note-95]

According to historian [Shaye J. D. Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaye_J._D._Cohen), the status of the offspring of mixed marriages was determined [patrilineally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrilineality) in the Bible. He brings two likely explanations for the change in [Mishnaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) times: first, the Mishnah may have been applying the same logic to mixed marriages as it had applied to other mixtures (*[Kil'ayim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kil'ayim_\(prohibition\))*). Thus, a mixed marriage is forbidden as is the union of a [horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse) and a [donkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donkey), and in both unions the offspring are judged matrilineally.[^cite_note-J.D._Cohen-96] Second, the [Tannaim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tannaim) may have been influenced by [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law), which dictated that when a parent could not contract a legal marriage, [offspring would follow the mother](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mater_semper_certa_est).[^cite_note-J.D._Cohen-96] Rabbi Rivon Krygier follows a similar reasoning, arguing that Jewish descent had formerly passed through the patrilineal descent and the law of matrilineal descent had its roots in the Roman legal system.[^cite_note-Klein2016-93]

## Origins

Further information: [Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan), [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites), [Yahwism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahwism), [Origins of Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origins_of_Judaism), and [History of ancient Israel and Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ancient_Israel_and_Judah)



Egyptian depiction of the visit of Western Asiatics in colorful garments, labeled as *[Aamu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aamu)*. The painting is from the tomb of a 12th dynasty official [Khnumhotep II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khnumhotep_II) at [Beni Hasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beni_Hasan), and dated to c. 1900 BCE. Their nearest Biblical contemporaries were the earliest of Hebrews, such as [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham) and [Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_\(Genesis\)).

[^cite_note-97]

[^cite_note-98]

[^cite_note-archaeology.org-99]

[^cite_note-100]

The prehistory and [ethnogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnogenesis) of the Jews are closely intertwined with archaeology, biology, historical textual records, mythology, and religious literature. The ethnic origin of the Jews lie in the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites), a confederation of Iron Age [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages)-speaking tribes that inhabited a part of [Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan) during the [tribal and monarchic periods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ancient_Israel_and_Judah).[^cite_note-101] Modern Jews are named after and also descended from the southern Israelite [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah).[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-Schama2014-103][^cite_note-104][^cite_note-Ostrer2012-105][^cite_note-Brenner2010-106][^cite_note-Adams1840-107]

[Gary A. Rendsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_A._Rendsburg) links the early Canaanite [nomadic pastoralists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic_pastoralism) confederation to the [Shasu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shasu) known to the Egyptians around the 15th century BCE.[^cite_note-auto1-108]

According to the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanakh) narrative, Jewish history begins with the [Biblical patriarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchs_\(Bible\)) such as [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham), his son [Isaac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac), Isaac's son [Jacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob), and the Biblical matriarchs [Sarah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah), [Rebecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_\(biblical_figure\)), [Leah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leah), and [Rachel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel), who lived in [Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan). The twelve sons of Jacob subsequently gave birth to the [Twelve Tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Tribes). Jacob and his family migrated to [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) after being invited to live with Jacob's son [Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_\(son_of_Jacob\)) by the [Pharaoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharaohs_in_the_Bible) himself. Jacob's descendants were later enslaved until the [Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Exodus), led by [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses). Afterwards, the Israelites conquered Canaan under Moses' successor [Joshua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua), and went through the period of the [Biblical judges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_judges) after the death of Joshua. Through the mediation of [Samuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel), the Israelites were subject to a king, [Saul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saul), who was succeeded by [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David) and then [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon), after whom the [United Monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(united_monarchy\)) ended and was split into a separate [Kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)) and a [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah). The Kingdom of Judah is described as comprising the tribes of [Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_of_Judah), [Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_of_Benjamin) and partially, [Levi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_of_Levi). They later assimilated remnants of other tribes who migrated there from the northern Kingdom of Israel.[^cite_note-Broshi_2001_174-109][^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111]

In the extra-biblical record, the Israelites become visible as a people between 1200 and 1000 BCE.[^cite_note-112] There is well accepted archeological evidence referring to "Israel" in the [Merneptah Stele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merneptah_Stele), which dates to about 1200 BCE,[^cite_note-NollMerneptah-113][^cite_note-ThompsonMerneptah-114] and in the [Mesha Stele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesha_Stele) from 840 BCE. It is debated whether a period like that of the [Biblical judges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_judges) occurred[^cite_note-Yoder2015-115][^cite_note-Brettler2002-116][^cite_note-Thompson2000-117][^cite_note-HjelmThompson2016-118][^cite_note-Davies1995-119] and if there ever was a [United Monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(united_monarchy\)).[^cite_note-lipschits-120][^cite_note-Finkelstein-121][^cite_note-Kuhrtp438-122][^cite_note-Wright-123] There is further disagreement about the earliest existence of the Kingdoms of Israel and Judah and their extent and power. Historians agree that a [Kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)) existed by c. 900 BCE,[^cite_note-Finkelstein-121]<sup>: 169–95 </sup>[^cite_note-Kuhrtp438-122][^cite_note-Wright-123] there is a consensus that a [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah) existed by c. 700 BCE at least,[^cite_note-Pitcher-124] and recent excavations in [Khirbet Qeiyafa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khirbet_Qeiyafa) have provided strong evidence for dating the Kingdom of Judah to the 10th century BCE.[^cite_note-125] In 587 BCE, [Nebuchadnezzar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebuchadnezzar_II), King of the [Neo-Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire), [besieged Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(587_BC\)), destroyed the [First Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple) and deported parts of the Judahite population.[^cite_note-us-israel-archaeology-126]

Scholars disagree regarding the extent to which the Bible should be accepted as a historical source for early Israelite history. [Rendsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_A._Rendsburg) states that there are two approximately equal groups of scholars who debate the [historicity of the biblical narrative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historicity_of_the_Bible), the [minimalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_minimalism) who largely reject it, and the [maximalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_maximalism) who largely accept it, with the minimalists being the more vocal of the two.[^cite_note-127]

&#x20;Some of the leading minimalists reframe the biblical account as constituting the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites)' inspiring [national myth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_myth) narrative, suggesting that according to the modern archaeological and historical account, the Israelites and their culture did not overtake the region by force, but instead branched out of the [Canaanite peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_languages) and culture through the development of a distinct [monolatristic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monolatrism)—and later [monotheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism)—religion of [Yahwism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahwism) centered on [Yahweh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahweh), one of the gods of the Canaanite pantheon. The growth of Yahweh-centric belief, along with a number of cultic practices, gradually gave rise to a distinct Israelite [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group), setting them apart from other Canaanites.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETubb199813–14-128][^cite_note-129][^cite_note-auto1-108] According to [Dever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_G._Dever), modern [archaeologists](18951655.md) have largely discarded the search for evidence of the biblical narrative surrounding the patriarchs and the exodus.[^cite_note-130]



Depiction of King [Jehu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehu), tenth [king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King) of the [northern Kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)), on the [Black Obelisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Obelisk) of [Shalmaneser III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalmaneser_III), 841–840 BCE.

[^cite_note-131]

&#x20;This is "the only portrayal we have in ancient Near Eastern art of an Israelite or Judaean monarch".

[^cite_note-132]

According to the maximalist position, the modern archaeological record independently points to a narrative which largely agrees with the biblical account. This narrative provides a testimony of the Israelites as a [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomad) people known to the [Egyptians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Kingdom_of_Egypt) as belonging to the [Shasu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BFApiru). Over time these nomads left the desert and [settled on the central mountain range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelite_highland_settlement) of the land of Canaan, in simple semi-nomadic settlements in which pig bones are notably absent. This population gradually shifted from a [tribal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe) lifestyle to a monarchy. While the archaeological record of the ninth century BCE provides evidence for two monarchies, one in the south under a dynasty founded by a figure named David with its capital in [Jerusalem](16043.md), and one in the north under a dynasty founded by a figure named [Omri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omri) with its capital in [Samaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaria_\(ancient_city\)). It also points to an early [monarchic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchic_period) in which these regions shared [material culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Material_culture) and religion, suggesting a common origin. Archaeological finds also provide evidence for the later cooperation of these two kingdoms in their coalition against [Aram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aram-Damascus), and for their destructions by the [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria) and later by the Babylonians.[^cite_note-auto1-108]

[Genetic studies on Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_studies_on_Jews) show that most Jews worldwide bear a common genetic heritage which originates in the [Middle East](19323.md), and that they share certain genetic traits with other Gentile peoples of the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent).[^cite_note-WhoAreTheJews-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] The genetic composition of different Jewish groups shows that Jews share a common gene pool dating back four millennia, as a marker of their common ancestral origin.[^cite_note-136] Despite their long-term separation, Jewish communities maintained their unique commonalities, propensities, and sensibilities in culture, tradition, and language.[^cite_note-137]

### Ancient Israel and Judah

Further information: [Ancient Israel and Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Israel_and_Judah)

The earliest recorded evidence of a people by the name of Israel appears in the [Merneptah Stele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merneptah_Stele), which dates to around 1200 BCE. The majority of scholars agree that this text refers to the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites), a group that inhabited the central highlands of [Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan), where archaeological evidence shows that hundreds of small [settlements were constructed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelite_highland_settlement) between the 12th and 10th centuries BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStager199891-138][^cite_note-139] The Israelites differentiated themselves from neighboring peoples through various distinct characteristics, including [religious practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahwism), [prohibition on intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamy), and an emphasis on genealogy and family history.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcNutt199935-140][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDever2003206-141]

In the 10th century BCE, two neighboring Israelite kingdoms—the northern [Kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)) and the southern [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah)—emerged. Since their inception, they shared ethnic, cultural, [linguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Hebrew) and [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahwism) characteristics despite a complicated relationship. Israel, with its capital mostly in [Samaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaria_\(ancient_city\)), was larger and wealthier, and soon developed into a regional power.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelsteinSilberman2002146–7-142] In contrast, Judah, with its capital in [Jerusalem](16043.md), was less prosperous and covered a smaller, mostly mountainous territory. However, while in Israel the royal succession was often decided by a military coup d'état, resulting in several dynasty changes, political stability in Judah was much greater, as it was ruled by the [House of David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davidic_line) for the whole four centuries of its existence.[^cite_note-143] Scholars also describe Biblical Jews as a 'proto-[nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation)', in the modern nationalist sense, comparable to [classical Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece), the [Gauls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauls) and the [British Celts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Iron_Age).[^cite_note-Smith-1993-144][^cite_note-Goodblatt-2006-145][^cite_note-auto-146][^cite_note-Hastings-1997-147]



Gate from the Israelite city of [Hazor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Hazor)'s royal fort, dating to the time of [Ahab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahab), 9th century BCE

Around 720 BCE, Kingdom of Israel was destroyed when it was [conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samerina) by the [Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire), which came to dominate the ancient Near East.[^cite_note-Broshi_2001_174-109] Under the [Assyrian resettlement policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resettlement_policy_of_the_Neo-Assyrian_Empire), a significant portion of the northern Israelite population was [exiled to Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_captivity) and replaced by immigrants from the same region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETobolowsky202269–70,_73–75-148] During the same period, and throughout the 7th century BCE, the Kingdom of Judah, now under Assyrian [vassalage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassal_state), experienced a period of prosperity and witnessed a significant population growth.[^cite_note-149] This prosperity continued until the Neo-Assyrian king [Sennacherib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sennacherib) [devastated the region of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sennacherib's_campaign_in_the_Levant) in response to a rebellion in the area, ultimately halting at [Jerusalem](16043.md).[^cite_note-150] Later in the same century, the Assyrians were defeated by the rising [Neo-Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire), and Judah became its vassal. In 587 BCE, following a [revolt in Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah's_revolts_against_Babylon), the Babylonian king Nebuchadnezzar II [besieged and destroyed Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(587_BC\)) and the [First Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple), putting an end to the kingdom.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipiński202094-151][^cite_note-152] Judah's population underwent a demographic collapse as a result of [deportations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_captivity) to [Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia), wartime deaths, famine, and the flight of inhabitants abroad.[^cite_note-153]

### Second Temple period

Main article: [Second Temple period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_period)

Further information: [Maccabean Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabean_Revolt), [Hasmonean Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_Judea), and [Jewish–Roman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_wars)

According to the [Book of Ezra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Ezra), the Persian [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) ended the Babylonian exile in 538 BCE,[^cite_note-rennert-154] the year after he captured Babylon.[^cite_note-155] The exile ended with the return under [Zerubbabel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zerubbabel) the Prince (so called because he was a descendant of the royal line of [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David)) and Joshua the Priest (a descendant of the line of the former [High Priests of the Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Priest_of_Israel)) and their construction of the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple) circa 521–516 BCE.[^cite_note-rennert-154] As part of the [Persian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire), the former Kingdom of Judah became the province of Judah (*[Yehud Medinata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehud_Medinata)*),[^cite_note-156] with a smaller territory[^cite_note-Grabbe355-157] and a reduced population.[^cite_note-Finkelstein-121]

&#x20;Judea was under control of the [Achaemenids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) until [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) conquered the region in 332 BCE. After his death, Judea passed between [rival Hellenistic kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diadochi); it was firmly held by [Ptolemaic Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom) from about 301 BCE and then by the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) from 198 BCE. Seleucid attempts under [Antiochus IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_IV_Epiphanes) to suppress Jewish religious practice provoked the [Maccabean Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabean_Revolt) (167–141 BCE), which resulted in an [independent Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_Judea); the Jews once again enjoyed political independence from 140 to 63 BCE.[^cite_note-BangScheidel2013-158] The [Hasmonean rulers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_dynasty), who descended from the [Maccabees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabees), progressively expanded their territories beyond the lands of the historical [kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah).[^cite_note-159] Under [John Hyrcanus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hyrcanus), [Aristobulus I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristobulus_I), and [Alexander Jannaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Jannaeus), the kingdom came to encompass [Samaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaria), [Idumaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idumaea), [Galilee](12639.md), and parts of [Transjordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transjordan_\(region\)); during these conquests, the Idumeans, who had infiltrated southern Judea after the destruction of the [First Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple), were converted to Judaism en masse.[^cite_note-Levin-2020-160][^cite_note-161] In 63 BCE, the Roman general [Pompey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompey) intervened in a [succession war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_civil_war) that had erupted following the death of [Salome Alexandra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Alexandra), and made Judea a [Roman client state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Client_kingdoms_in_ancient_Rome). Rome subsequently installed the [Herodian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodian_dynasty) as vassal kings, with [Herod the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great) ruling from 37 to 4 BCE; after his death, the [kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodian_kingdom) was [divided among his sons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodian_tetrarchy), and in 6 CE Judea proper was placed under direct [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) administration as the [province of Judaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_\(Roman_province\)).[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-Malamat1976-163]



[Temple menorah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_menorah) depicted on a coin of the [Hasmonean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_dynasty) king of Judaea [Antigonus II Mattathias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antigonus_II_Mattathias)

&#x20;The [Jewish–Roman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_wars), a series of failed uprisings against Roman rule during the first and second centuries CE, had profound and devastating consequences for the Jewish population of Judaea.[^cite_note-Zissu-2018-164][^cite_note-FahlbuschBromiley2005-165] The [First Jewish–Roman War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_War) (66–73/74 CE) culminated in the [destruction of Jerusalem and the Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)), after which the significantly diminished Jewish population was stripped of political autonomy.[^cite_note-AHJ-GM-166] A few generations later, the [Bar Kokhba Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_Kokhba_Revolt) (132–136 CE) erupted in response to Roman plans to rebuild Jerusalem as a [Roman colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aelia_Capitolina), and, possibly, to restrictions on circumcision.[^cite_note-167] Its violent suppression by the Romans led to the near-total depopulation of Judea,[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-Levine-2017-169] and the demographic and cultural center of Jewish life shifted to [Galilee](12639.md).[^cite_note-Levine-2017-169] Jews were subsequently banned from residing in Jerusalem and the surrounding area,[^cite_note-170] and the province of Judaea was renamed [Syria Palaestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Palaestina).[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] These developments effectively ended Jewish efforts to restore political sovereignty in the region for nearly two millennia.[^cite_note-173] Similar upheavals impacted the Jewish communities in the empire's eastern provinces during the [Diaspora Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora_Revolt) (115–117 CE), leading to the near-total destruction of Jewish diaspora communities in [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Libya), [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Cyprus) and [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Egypt),[^cite_note-Kerkeslager-2006-174][^cite_note-Zeev-2006-175] including the highly influential community in [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria).[^cite_note-AHJ-GM-166][^cite_note-Kerkeslager-2006-174]



A Roman coin inscribed *[Ivdaea Capta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_Capta_coinage),* or "captive Judea" (71 CE), representing Judea as a seated mourning woman (right), and a Jewish captive with hands tied (left)

The destruction of the Second Temple in 70 CE brought profound changes to Judaism. With the Temple's central place in Jewish worship gone, religious practices shifted towards [prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_prayer), [Torah study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah_study) (including [Oral Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_Torah)), and communal gatherings in [synagogues](48559.md). Judaism also lost much of its [sectarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarian) nature.[^cite_note-Magness-176]<sup>: 69 </sup> Two of the three main sects that flourished during the late Second Temple period, namely the [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees) and [Essenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essenes), eventually disappeared, while [Pharisaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees) beliefs became the foundational, liturgical, and ritualistic basis of [Rabbinic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism), which emerged as the prevailing form of Judaism since late antiquity.[^cite_note-Karesh-2006-177]

### Babylon and Rome

Further information: [Rabbinic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_period), [History of the Jews in the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Roman_Empire), and [Talmudic academies in Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmudic_academies_in_Babylonia)



[Masada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masada) fortress overlooking the [Dead Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Sea), the location of a [1st-century Roman siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Masada)

The [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) existed well before the destruction of the Second Temple in 70 CE and had been ongoing for centuries, with the dispersal driven by both forced expulsions and voluntary migrations.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-AHJ-GM-166] In Mesopotamia, a testimony to the beginnings of the Jewish community can be found in [Joachin's ration tablets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehoiachin's_Rations_Tablets), listing provisions allotted to the exiled Judean king and his family by [Nebuchadnezzar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebuchadnezzar_II), and further evidence are the [Al-Yahudu Tablets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Yahudu_Tablets), dated to the 6th–5th centuries BCE and related to the exiles from Judea arriving after the destruction of the [First Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple),[^cite_note-us-israel-archaeology-126] though there is ample evidence for the presence of Jews in Babylonia even from 626 BCE.[^cite_note-179] In Egypt, the [documents from Elephantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephantine_papyri_and_ostraca) reveal the trials of a community founded by a Persian Jewish garrison at two fortresses on the frontier during the 5th–4th centuries BCE, and according to [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) the Jewish community in Alexandria existed since the founding of the city in the 4th century BCE by [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great).[^cite_note-180] By 200 BCE, there were well established Jewish communities both in [Egypt](8087628.md) and [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) ("[Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Iraq)" in Jewish sources) and in the two centuries that followed, Jewish populations were also present in [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia), [Greece](12108.md), [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(ancient_kingdom\)), [Cyrene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrene,_Libya), and, beginning in the middle of the first century BCE, in the city of [Rome](25458.md).[^cite_note-Smallwood-181][^cite_note-AHJ-GM-166] Later, in the first centuries CE, as a result of the [Jewish–Roman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_wars), a large number of Jews were taken as captives, sold into slavery, or compelled to flee from the regions affected by the wars, contributing to the formation and expansion of Jewish communities across the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) as well as in [Arabia](47858.md)[^cite_note-182] and Mesopotamia.

After the Bar Kokhba Revolt, the significantly reduced Jewish population in Judaea made efforts to recover from the revolt's devastating effects, but never fully regained its former strength.[^cite_note-Ehrlich-2022-183][^cite_note-184] Between the second and fourth centuries CE, the region of Galilee emerged as the primary center of Jewish life in Syria Palaestina, experiencing both demographic growth and cultural development. It was during this period that two central rabbinic texts, the *[Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah)* and the *[Jerusalem Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Talmud)*, were composed.[^cite_note-Leibner-2009-185] The Romans recognized the patriarchs—rabbinic sages such as [Judah ha-Nasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_ha-Nasi)—as representatives of the Jewish people, granting them a certain degree of autonomy.[^cite_note-186] However, as the Roman Empire gave way to the [Christianized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_Christianization_of_the_Roman_Empire) Byzantine Empire under [Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great), Jews began to face persecution by both the Church and imperial authorities, Jews came to be persecuted by the church and the authorities, and many immigrated to communities in the diaspora. By the fourth century CE, Jews are believed to have lost their demographic majority in Syria Palaestina.[^cite_note-Kessler20102-187][^cite_note-Ehrlich-2022-183]

The long-established Jewish community of Mesopotamia, which had been living under [Parthian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire) and later [Sasanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) rule, beyond the confines of the Roman Empire, became an important center of [Jewish study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_studies) as Judea's Jewish population declined.[^cite_note-Kessler20102-187][^cite_note-Ehrlich-2022-183] Estimates often place the Babylonian Jewish community of the 3rd to 7th centuries at around one million, making it the largest Jewish diaspora community of that period.[^cite_note-Gafni-2006-188] Under the political leadership of the [exilarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exilarch), who was regarded as a royal heir of the House of David, this community had an autonomous status and served as a place of refuge for the Jews of [Syria Palaestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Palaestina). A number of significant [Talmudic academies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmudic_academies_in_Babylonia), such as the [Nehardea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehardea_Academy), [Pumbedita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pumbedita_Academy), and [Sura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sura_Academy) academies, were established in Mesopotamia, and many important *[Amoraim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amoraim)* were active there. The [Babylonian Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_Talmud), a centerpiece of Jewish religious law, was compiled in Babylonia in the 3rd to 6th centuries.[^cite_note-189]

### Middle Ages

Further information: [History of European Jews in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_European_Jews_in_the_Middle_Ages), [Mizrahi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews), and [Sephardi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews)

Jewish diaspora communities are generally described to have [coalesced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalescent_theory) into three major [ethnic subdivisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions) according to where their ancestors settled: the *[Ashkenazim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews)* (initially in the Rhineland and France), the *[Sephardim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews)* (initially in the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_and_Portuguese_Jews), sometimes includes [North Africa](21714.md)), and the *[Mizrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews)* ([Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_under_Muslim_rule) and sometimes includes [North Africa](21714.md)).[^cite_note-190] [Romaniote Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews), [Cochin Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochin_Jews), \[Ethiopian Jews]] also predated the arrival of the Sephardic diaspora.[^cite_note-191]

During the same period, Jewish communities in the Middle East thrived under Islamic rule, especially in cities like [Baghdad](4492.md), [Cairo](6293.md), and [Damascus](8914.md). In Babylonia, from the 7th to 11th centuries, the [Pumbedita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pumbedita_Academy) and [Sura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sura_Academy) academies led the Arab and, to an extent, the entire Jewish world. The deans and students of said academies defined the [Geonic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geonim) in Jewish history.[^cite_note-192] From the 9th century onward, Jews increasingly became specialized in urban occupations such as trade, finance, and medicine. One explanation attributes this to antisemitic restrictions imposed upon Jews by local rulers. [Simon Kuznets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Kuznets) argued that urbanization was an internal strategy for maintaining cultural cohesion. Knowledge of Hebrew provided a shared written language across communities with different spoken languages. The high level of literacy among Jewish farmers gave them an advantage in the skilled occupations in expanding urban centers such as Baghdad and by the growth of long-distance trade between Asia, the Muslim world, and western Europe.[^cite_note-193] Following this period were the [Rishonim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishonim) who lived from the 11th to 15th centuries. Like their European counterparts, Jews in the Middle East and North Africa also faced periods of persecution and discriminatory policies, with the [Almohad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohad_Caliphate) in [North Africa](21714.md) and [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) issuing forced conversion decrees, causing Jews such as [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides) to seek safety in other regions.

Despite experiencing repeated waves of persecution, Ashkenazi Jews in Western Europe worked in a variety of fields, making an impact on their communities' economy and societies. In [Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francia), for example, figures like [Isaac Judaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_the_Jew) and [Armentarius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armentarius_\(moneylender\)) occupied prominent social and economic positions. Francia also witnessed the development of a sophisticated tradition of biblical commentary, as exemplified by [Rashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashi) and the [*tosafists*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tosafot). In 1144, the [first documented blood libel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_of_Norwich) occurred in [Norwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwich), England, marking an escalation in the pattern of discrimination and violence that Jews had already been subjected to throughout medieval Europe.[^cite_note-194] During the 12th and 13th centuries, Jews faced frequent antisemitic legislation - including laws prescribing distinctive dress - alongside segregation, repeated [blood libels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_libel), [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom), and massacres such as the [Rhineland Massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhineland_massacres) (1066). The Jews of the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire) were designated [Servi camerae regis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Servi_camerae_regis) (“servants of the imperial chamber”) by [Frederick II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor), a status that afforded limited protection while simultaneously entangling them in the political struggles between the emperor and the German principalities and cities. [Persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews_during_the_Black_Death) intensified during the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) in the mid-14th century, when Jews were accused of poisoning wells and many communities were destroyed.[^cite_note-195] These pressures, combined with major expulsions such as that from [England in 1290](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Expulsion), gradually pushed Ashkenazi Jewish populations eastward into Poland, Lithuania, and Russia.[^cite_note-196]



Hebrew inscription in the [Córdoba Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba_Synagogue), [Spain](26667.md) (1315)

One of the largest Jewish communities of the Middle Ages was in the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula), which for a time contained the largest Jewish population in Europe.[^cite_note-197] Iberian Jewry endured discrimination under the [Visigoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigothic_Kingdom) but saw its fortunes improve under [Umayyad rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_state_of_C%C3%B3rdoba) and later the [Taifa kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taifa). During this period, the Jews of [Muslim Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus) entered a "[Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Jewish_culture_in_Spain)" marked by achievements in [Hebrew poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_poetry_from_al-Andalus) and literature, religious scholarship, grammar, medicine and science, with leading figures including [Hasdai ibn Shaprut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasdai_ibn_Shaprut), [Judah Halevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_Halevi), [Moses ibn Ezra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_ibn_Ezra) and [Solomon ibn Gabirol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_ibn_Gabirol).[^cite_note-Gerber-2021-198] Jews also rose to high office, most notably [Samuel ibn Naghrillah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_ibn_Naghrillah), a scholar and poet who served as grand vizier and military commander of [Granada](38264.md).[^cite_note-Gerber-2021-198] The Golden Age ended with the rise of the radical [Almoravid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravid_dynasty) and [Almohad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohad_Caliphate) dynasties, whose persecutions drove many Jews from Iberia (including [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides)),[^cite_note-Gerber-2021-198] together with the advancing [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista). In 1391, [widespread pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_of_1391) swept across Spain, leaving thousands dead and forcing mass conversions.[^cite_note-Tov_Assis-2018-199] The [Spanish Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition) was later established to pursue, torture and execute *[conversos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Converso)* who continued to practice Judaism in secret, while public [disputations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disputation) were staged to discredit Judaism.[^cite_note-Tov_Assis-2018-199] In 1492, after the Reconquista, [Isabella I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_I_of_Castile) of [Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Castile) and [Ferdinand II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon) of [Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aragon) decreed the [expulsion of all Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Jews_from_Spain) who refused conversion, sending an estimated 200,000 into exile in [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal), Italy, [North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb), and the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md).[^cite_note-Beinart-2001-200] In 1497, [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal)'s Jews, about 30,000, were formally ordered expelled but instead were forcibly converted to retain their economic role.[^cite_note-Tov_Assis-2018-199] In 1498, some 3,500 Jews were expelled from [Navarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navarre).[^cite_note-Tov_Assis-2018-199] Many converts outwardly adopted Christianity while secretly preserving Jewish practices, becoming [crypto-Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crypto-Judaism) (also known as [*marranos*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marrano) or *[anusim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anusim)*), who remained targets of the various Inquisitions for centuries.[^cite_note-201]

### Early modern period

Further information: [Ottoman Jewry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Jewry), [Hasidic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism), [Misnagdim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misnagdim), and [Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah)

Following the expulsions from Spain and Portugal in the 1490s, Jewish exiles dispersed across the Mediterranean, Europe, and North Africa. Many settled in the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md)—which, replacing the Iberian Peninsula, became home to the world's largest Jewish population—where new communities developed in [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia), the [Balkans](4829.md), and the Land of Israel.[^cite_note-Hacker-2017-202] Cities such as [Istanbul](3391396.md) and [Thessaloniki](40471.md) grew into major Jewish centers, while in 16th-century [Safed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safed) a flourishing spiritual life took shape.[^cite_note-Hacker-2017-202] There, [Solomon Alkabetz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_Alkabetz), [Moses Cordovero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_Cordovero), and [Isaac Luria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Luria) developed influential new schools of [Kabbalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah), giving powerful impetus to Jewish mysticism, and [Joseph Karo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Karo) composed the *[Shulchan Aruch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulchan_Aruch)*, which became a cornerstone of Jewish law.[^cite_note-Hacker-2017-202] In the 17th century, Portuguese *conversos* who returned to Judaism and engaged in trade and banking helped establish [Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Amsterdam) as a prosperous Jewish center,[^cite_note-203] while also forming communities in cities such as [Antwerp](32149462.md) and [London](17867.md).[^cite_note-Endelman-2017-204] This period also witnessed waves of messianic fervor, most notably the rise of the [Sabbatean movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabbateans) in the 1660s, led by [Sabbatai Zvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabbatai_Zevi) of [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir), which reverberated throughout the Jewish world.[^cite_note-205]



Interior of the [Portuguese Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Synagogue_\(Amsterdam\)) in [Amsterdam](844.md), painted by [Emanuel de Witte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_de_Witte), c. 1680, Rijksmuseum

In Eastern Europe, [Poland–Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth) became the principal center of Ashkenazi Jewry, eventually becoming home to the largest Jewish population in the world. Jewish life flourished there in the early modern era, supported by relative stability, economic opportunity, and strong communal institutions.[^cite_note-Guesnet-2017-206] The mid-17th century brought devastation with the [Cossack uprisings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_uprisings) in Ukraine, which reversed migration flows and sent refugees westward, yet Poland–Lithuania remained the demographic and cultural heartland of Ashkenazic Jewry.[^cite_note-Guesnet-2017-206] Following the [partitions of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland), most of its Jews came under Russian rule and were confined to the "[Pale of Settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pale_of_Settlement)."[^cite_note-207] The 18th century also witnessed new religious and intellectual currents. [Hasidism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism), founded by [Baal Shem Tov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baal_Shem_Tov), emphasized mysticism and piety,[^cite_note-Dynner-2017-208] while its opponents, the [Misnagdim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misnagdim) ("opponents") led by the [Vilna Gaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilna_Gaon), defended rabbinic scholarship and tradition.[^cite_note-Dynner-2017-208]

In Western Europe, during the 1760s and 1770s, the [Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah) (Jewish [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment)) emerged in German-speaking lands, where figures such as [Moses Mendelssohn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_Mendelssohn) promoted secular learning, vernacular literacy, and integration into European society.[^cite_note-209] Elsewhere, Jews began to be re-admitted to Western Europe, including England, where [Menasseh ben Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menasseh_Ben_Israel) petitioned [Oliver Cromwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Cromwell) for their return.[^cite_note-Endelman-2017-204]

In the Americas, Jews of Sephardic descent first arrived as *conversos* in Spanish and Portuguese colonies, where many faced trial by [Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquisition) tribunals for "judaizing."[^cite_note-Klooster-2017-210] A more durable presence began in [Dutch Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Brazil), where Jews openly practiced their religion and established the first synagogues in the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World), before the Portuguese reconquest forced their dispersal to Amsterdam, the [Caribbean](18956035.md), and [North America](21139.md).[^cite_note-Klooster-2017-210] Sephardic communities took root in [Curaçao](65205.md), [Suriname](26828.md), [Jamaica](15660.md), and [Barbados](3455.md), later joined by Ashkenazi migrants.[^cite_note-Klooster-2017-210] In North America, Jews were present from the mid-17th century, with [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam) hosting the first organized congregation in 1654.[^cite_note-Pencak-2017-211] By the time of the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution), small communities in [New York](645042.md), [Newport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newport,_Rhode_Island), [Philadelphia](50585.md), [Savannah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savannah,_Georgia), and [Charleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina) played an active role in the struggle for independence.[^cite_note-Pencak-2017-211]

### Modern period and the State of Israel

Further information: [Jewish emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_emancipation), [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism), [The Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust), and [History of Israel (1948–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Israel_\(1948%E2%80%93present\))

In the late 19th century, Jews in Western Europe gradually achieved legal [emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_emancipation), though social acceptance remained limited by persistent antisemitism and rising nationalism. In Eastern Europe, particularly within the Russian Empire's Pale of Settlement, Jews faced mounting legal restrictions and recurring [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom). From this environment emerged [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism), a national revival movement originating in Central and Eastern Europe that sought to re-establish a Jewish polity in the Land of Israel as a means of returning the Jewish people to their ancestral homeland and ending centuries of exile and persecution. This led to waves of Jewish migration to [Ottoman Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Palestine). [Theodor Herzl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodor_Herzl), who is considered the father of [political Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Types_of_Zionism#Political_Zionism),[^cite_note-212] offered his vision of a future Jewish state in his 1896 book *[Der Judenstaat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Judenstaat)* (*The Jewish State*); a year later, he presided over the [First Zionist Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Zionist_Congress).[^cite_note-213]

The antisemitism that inflicted Jewish communities in Europe also triggered a mass exodus of 2.8 million Jews to the [United States](3434750.md) between 1881 and 1924.[^cite_note-214] Despite this, some Jews of Europe and the United States were able to make great achievements in various fields of science and culture. Among the most influential from this period are [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein) in physics, [Sigmund Freud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmund_Freud) in psychology, [Franz Kafka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Kafka) in literature, and [Irving Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irving_Berlin) in music. Many [Nobel Prize](21201.md) winners at this time were Jewish, as is still the case.[^cite_note-Jewish_Nobel_Prize_Winners-215]



Map of the Jewish diaspora:\
&#x20;

&#x20; Israel

&#x20; + 1,000,000

&#x20; + 100,000

&#x20; + 10,000

&#x20; + 1,000

When [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) and the [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) came to power in [Germany](21212.md) in 1933, the situation for Jews deteriorated rapidly as a direct result of Nazi policies. Many Jews fled from Europe to [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), the United States, and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) as a result of racially [antisemitic laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Jewish_legislation_in_pre-war_Nazi_Germany), and economic difficulties. [World War II](32927.md) started in 1939, and by 1941 Germany occupied much of Europe. Following its [invasion of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa) in 1941, Germany escalated its persecution of Jews into the [Final Solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Solution): an extensively organised effort to exterminate all Jews in Europe, implemented through [mass shootings](31883778.md), [deportations](412990.md), [concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration_camp), and [death camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_camp).[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217] In Poland, three million were murdered in [gas chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_chambers) in all concentration camps combined, with one million at the [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz) camp complex alone. In total, approximately six million Jews were systemically murdered in the [genocide](12441.md) known as [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust),[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] by both the German state and other governments allied to or occupied by the Nazis.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] Jews actively resisted this genocide militantly, such as in the [Warsaw Ghetto Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Ghetto_Uprising).

During this period, many Jews emigrated to Mandatory Palestine. In 1944, the [Jewish insurgency in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_insurgency_in_Mandatory_Palestine) began. On 14 May 1948, at the end of the mandate, [David Ben-Gurion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ben-Gurion) declared the creation of the [State of Israel](9282173.md), a "[Jewish and democratic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_and_democratic_state)". The [1948 Arab-Israeli Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab-Israeli_War) followed. In 1949, the war ended, and Israel began building its state and absorbing waves of *[Aliyah](351513.md)*, granting [citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizenship) to Jews worldwide through the [Law of Return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Return) passed in 1950. However, ongoing [Arab–Israeli conflicts](7960202.md) continued.

## Culture

Main article: [Jewish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture)

### Religion

Main article: [Judaism](15624.md)

See also: [Jewish atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_atheism) and [Jewish secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_secularism)

The Jewish [people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_peoplehood) and the religion of [Judaism](15624.md) are strongly interrelated. [Converts to Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism) have a status within the Jewish people equal to those born into it.[^cite_note-223] However, converts who go on to practice no Judaism are likely to be viewed with skepticism.[^cite_note-224] Mainstream Judaism does not [proselytize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselytism), and conversion is considered a difficult task. A significant portion of conversions are undertaken by children of mixed marriages, or would-be or current spouses of Jews.[^cite_note-225]

The [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible), a religious interpretation of the traditions and early history of the Jews, established the first of the [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions), which are now practiced by 54 percent of the world. Judaism guides its adherents in both practice and belief, and has been called not only a religion, but also a "way of life,"[^cite_note-226] which has made drawing a clear distinction between Judaism, [Jewish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture), and [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity) rather difficult. Throughout history, in eras and places as diverse as the ancient [Hellenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) world,[^cite_note-227] in Europe before and after [The Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Age_of_Enlightenment) (see [Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah)),[^cite_note-228] in [Islamic Spain and Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus),[^cite_note-Sharot2930-229] in [North Africa](21714.md) and the [Middle East](19323.md),[^cite_note-Sharot2930-229] [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Jews),[^cite_note-230] [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_China),[^cite_note-231] or the contemporary [United States](1131183.md)[^cite_note-232] and [Israel](9282173.md),[^cite_note-233] cultural phenomena have developed that are in some sense characteristically Jewish without being at all specifically religious. Some factors in this come from within Judaism, others from the interaction of Jews or specific communities of Jews with their surroundings, and still others from the inner social and cultural dynamics of the community, as opposed to from the religion itself. This phenomenon has led to considerably different [Jewish cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture) unique to their own communities.[^cite_note-234]

### Languages

Main article: [Jewish languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_languages)

[Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) is the [liturgical language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_language) of Judaism (termed *lashon ha-kodesh*, "the holy tongue"), the language in which most of the Hebrew scriptures ([Tanakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanakh)) were composed, and the daily speech of the Jewish people for centuries. By the 5th century BCE, [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic_language), a closely related tongue, joined Hebrew as the spoken language in [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea).[^cite_note-Grintz-235] By the 3rd century BCE, some Jews of the diaspora were speaking [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek).[^cite_note-236] Others, such as in the Jewish communities of [Asoristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asoristan), known to Jews as Babylonia, were speaking Hebrew and [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Babylonian_Aramaic), the languages of the [Babylonian Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_Talmud). Dialects of these same languages were also used by the Jews of [Syria Palaestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Palaestina) at that time.

For centuries, Jews worldwide have spoken the local or dominant languages of the regions they migrated to, often developing distinctive [dialectal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialect) forms or branches that became independent languages. [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_language) is the Judaeo-German language developed by [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews) who migrated to [Central Europe](5188.md). [Ladino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaeo-Spanish) is the Judaeo-Spanish language developed by [Sephardic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) Jews who migrated to the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula). Due to many factors, including the impact of [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) on European Jewry, the [Jewish exodus from Arab and Muslim countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_exodus_from_Arab_and_Muslim_countries), and widespread emigration from other Jewish communities around the world, ancient and distinct [Jewish languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_languages) of several communities, including [Judaeo-Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaeo-Georgian), [Judaeo-Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Arabic), [Judeo-Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Persian), [Judeo-Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Berber_language), [Krymchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krymchak_language), [Judeo-Malayalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Malayalam) and many others, have largely fallen out of use.[^cite_note-Languages-7]

For over sixteen centuries, Hebrew was used almost exclusively as a liturgical language, and as the language in which most books had been written on Judaism, with a few speaking only Hebrew on the [Sabbath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat).[^cite_note-237] Hebrew was [revived as a spoken language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revival_of_the_Hebrew_language) by [Eliezer ben Yehuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliezer_ben_Yehuda), who arrived in [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)) in 1881. It had not been used as a [mother tongue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_tongue) since [Tannaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tannaim) times.[^cite_note-Grintz-235] [Modern Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Hebrew) is designated as the "State language" of Israel.[^cite_note-238]

Despite efforts to revive Hebrew as the language of communication of the Jewish people, fluency in Hebrew is not widely held among Jews of the diaspora, and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) has emerged as the [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) of Jews worldwide.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] Although many Jews once had sufficient knowledge of Hebrew to study the classic literature, and [Jewish languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_languages) like [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish) and [Ladino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaeo-Spanish) were commonly used as recently as the early 20th century, most Jews lack such knowledge today and English has by and large superseded most Jewish vernaculars. The three most commonly spoken languages among Jews today are Hebrew, English, and [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language).
