Some [Romance languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_languages), particularly [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) and [Spanish](26825.md), are also widely used.[^cite_note-Languages-7] Yiddish has been spoken by more Jews in history than any other language,[^cite_note-244] but it is far less used today following [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) and the adoption of [Modern Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Hebrew) by the [Zionist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) and the [State of Israel](9282173.md). In some places, the mother language of the Jewish community differs from that of the general population or the dominant group. For example, in [Quebec](7954867.md), the Ashkenazic majority has adopted English, while the Sephardic minority uses French as its primary language.[^cite_note-forward-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] Similarly, [South African Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_South_Africa) adopted English rather than [Afrikaans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans).[^cite_note-248] Due to both Czarist and Soviet policies,[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] Russian has superseded Yiddish as the language of [Russian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Russia), but these policies have also affected neighboring communities.[^cite_note-251] Today, Russian is the first language for many Jewish communities in a number of [Post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states), such as [Ukraine](31750.md)[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-google-253][^cite_note-google2-254][^cite_note-255] and [Uzbekistan](31853.md),[^cite_note-jewishvirtuallibrary2-256] as well as for Ashkenazic Jews in [Azerbaijan](746.md),[^cite_note-Maoz-257][^cite_note-258] Georgia,[^cite_note-259] and [Tajikistan](30108.md).[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] Although communities in [North Africa](21714.md) today are small and dwindling, Jews there had shifted from a multilingual group to a monolingual one (or nearly so), speaking French in [Algeria](358.md),[^cite_note-262] [Morocco](19291.md),[^cite_note-Maoz-257] and the city of [Tunis](57659.md),[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-jdc-264] while most North Africans continue to use [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) or Berber as their mother tongue.

### Leadership

Main article: [Jewish leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_leadership)

There is no single governing body for the Jewish community, nor is there a single authority responsible for religious doctrine.[^cite_note-265] Instead, a variety of secular and religious institutions at the local, national, and international levels lead various parts of the Jewish community on a variety of issues.[^cite_note-266] Today, many countries have a [Chief Rabbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Rabbi) who serves as a representative of that country's Jewry. Although many [Hasidic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism) follow a certain hereditary [Hasidic dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hasidic_dynasties), there is no one commonly accepted leader of all Hasidic Jews. Many Jews believe that the [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism) will act as a unifying leader for Jews and the entire world.[^cite_note-267]

### Theories on ancient Jewish national identity



Bible manuscript in Hebrew, 14th century. Hebrew language and alphabet were the cornerstones of the Jewish national identity in antiquity.

A number of modern scholars of nationalism support the existence of Jewish [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_identity) in antiquity. One of them is David Goodblatt,[^cite_note-268] who generally believes in the existence of nationalism before the modern period. In his view, the Bible, the parabiblical literature, and Jewish national history provide the basis for a Jewish collective identity. Although many of the ancient Jews were illiterate (as were their neighbors), their national narrative was reinforced through public readings. The Hebrew language also constructed and preserved national identity. Although it was not widely spoken after the 5th century BCE, Goodblatt states:[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

> the mere presence of the language in spoken or written form could invoke the concept of a Jewish national identity. Even if one knew no Hebrew or was illiterate, one could recognize that a group of signs was in Hebrew script. ... It was the language of the Israelite ancestors, the national literature, and the national religion. As such it was inseparable from the national identity. Indeed its mere presence in visual or aural medium could invoke that identity.

[Anthony D. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_D._Smith), an historical sociologist considered one of the founders of the field of [nationalism studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism_studies), wrote that the Jews of the late Second Temple period provide "a closer approximation to the ideal type of the [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation) \[...] than perhaps anywhere else in the ancient world." He adds that this observation "must make us wary of pronouncing too readily against the possibility of the nation, and even a form of [religious nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_nationalism), before the onset of modernity."[^cite_note-Smith-1993-144] Smith located the formation of a Jewish nation as early as the 7th century BCE, under kings such as [Hezekiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezekiah) and [Josiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josiah), but argues that Jewish nationalism only emerged with the [Zealots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealots) and during the Bar Kokhba Revolt.[^cite_note-272] Agreeing with Smith, Goodblatt suggests omitting the qualifier "religious" from Smith's definition of ancient Jewish nationalism, noting that, according to Smith, a religious component in national memories and culture is common even in the modern era.[^cite_note-Goodblatt-2006-145] This view is echoed by political scientist [Tom Garvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Garvin), who writes that "something strangely like modern nationalism is documented for many peoples in medieval times and in classical times as well," citing the ancient Jews as one of several "obvious examples", alongside the [classical Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece) and the [Gaulish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauls) and [British Celts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Britons).[^cite_note-auto-146]

[Fergus Millar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fergus_Millar) suggests that the sources of Jewish national identity and their early nationalist movements in the first and second centuries CE included several key elements: the Bible as both a national history and legal source, the Hebrew language as a national language, a system of law, and social institutions such as schools, synagogues, and Sabbath worship.[^cite_note-273] [Adrian Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Hastings) argued that Jews are the "true proto-nation", that through the model of ancient Israel found in the Hebrew Bible, provided the world with the original concept of nationhood which later influenced Christian nations. However, following [Jerusalem's destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)) in the first century CE, Jews ceased to be a political entity and did not resemble a traditional nation-state for almost two millennia. Despite this, they maintained their national identity through collective memory, religion and sacred texts, even without land or political power, and remained a nation rather than just an ethnic group, eventually leading to the rise of [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) and the establishment of Israel.[^cite_note-Hastings-1997-147]

[Steven Weitzman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Weitzman_\(scholar\)) suggests that Jewish nationalist sentiment in antiquity was encouraged because, under foreign rule (Persians, Greeks, Romans), Jews could claim to be an ancient nation. This claim was based on the preservation and reverence of their scriptures, the Hebrew language, the Temple and priesthood, and other traditions of their ancestors.[^cite_note-274] [Doron Mendels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doron_Mendels) further observes that the Hasmonean kingdom, one of the few examples of indigenous statehood at its time, significantly reinforced Jewish national consciousness. The memory of this period of independence contributed to the persistent efforts to revive Jewish sovereignty in Judea, leading to the major revolts against Roman rule in the 1st and 2nd centuries CE.[^cite_note-275]

## Demographics

Further information: [Jewish population by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population_by_country)

### Ethnic divisions

Main article: [Jewish ethnic divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions)

Within the world's [Jewish population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population) there are distinct ethnic divisions, most of which are primarily the result of geographic branching from an originating [Israelite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelite) population, and subsequent independent evolutions. An array of Jewish communities was established by Jewish settlers in various places around the [Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World), often at great distances from one another, resulting in effective and often long-term isolation. During the [millennia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennia) of the [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) the communities would develop under the influence of their local environments: [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics), [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture), [natural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature), and populational. Today, manifestations of these differences among the Jews can be observed in [Jewish cultural expressions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture) of each community, including [Jewish linguistic diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_languages), culinary preferences, liturgical practices, religious interpretations, as well as degrees and sources of [genetic admixture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_admixture).[^cite_note-276]



[Maurycy Gottlieb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurycy_Gottlieb)'s [1878 painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_Praying_in_the_Synagogue_on_Yom_Kippur) of Ashkenazi Jews praying in synagogue on Yom Kippur.



Sephardic Jewish festival in [Tétouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%A9touan), Morocco, [Alfred Dehodencq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Dehodencq), 1865, [Paris Museum of Jewish Art and History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Art_et_d'Histoire_du_Juda%C3%AFsme)

Jews are often identified as belonging to one of two major groups: the *[Ashkenazim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews)* and the *[Sephardim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews)*. Ashkenazim are so named in reference to their geographical origins (their ancestors' culture coalesced in the [Rhineland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews#etymology), an area historically referred to by Jews as [Ashkenaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenaz)). Similarly, Sephardim ([Sefarad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sefarad) meaning "[Spain](26667.md)" in Hebrew) are named in reference their origins in [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_peninsula). The diverse groups of Jews of the Middle East and North Africa are often collectively referred to as *Sephardim* together with Sephardim proper for liturgical reasons having to do with their [prayer rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusach_\(Jewish_custom\)). A common term for many of these non-Spanish Jews who are sometimes still broadly grouped as Sephardim is *[Mizrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews)* (lit. 'easterners' in Hebrew). Nevertheless, Mizrahis and Sepharadim are usually ethnically distinct.[^cite_note-277]

Smaller groups include, but are not restricted to, [Indian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_India) such as the [Bene Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bene_Israel), [Bnei Menashe](1674436.md), [Cochin Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochin_Jews), and [Bene Ephraim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bene_Ephraim); the [Romaniotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews) of Greece; the [Italian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_rite_Jews) ("Italkim" or "Bené Roma"); the [Teimanim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teimanim) from [Yemen](350939.md); various [African Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_and_Judaism_in_Africa), including most numerously the [Beta Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_Israel) of [Ethiopia](187749.md); and [Chinese Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_China), most notably the [Kaifeng Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaifeng_Jews), as well as various other distinct but now almost extinct communities.[^cite_note-EJ571-278]

The divisions between all these groups are approximate and their boundaries are not always clear. The Mizrahim for example, are a heterogeneous collection of [North African](21714.md), [Central Asian](6742.md), [Caucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_\(geographic_region\)), and Middle Eastern Jewish communities that are no closer related to each other than they are to any of the earlier mentioned Jewish groups. In modern usage, however, the Mizrahim are sometimes termed *Sephardi* due to similar styles of liturgy, despite independent development from Sephardim proper. Thus, among Mizrahim there are [Egyptian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Jews), [Iraqi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Jews), [Lebanese Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Jews), [Kurdish Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_Jews), [Moroccan Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moroccan_Jews), [Libyan Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Jews), [Syrian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Jews), [Bukharian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukharian_Jews), [Mountain Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_Jews), [Georgian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Jews), [Iranian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Jews), [Afghan Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Jews), and various others. The [Teimanim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teimanim) from [Yemen](350939.md) are sometimes included, although their style of liturgy is unique and they differ in respect to the admixture found among them to that found in Mizrahim. In addition, there is a differentiation made between Sephardi migrants who established themselves in the [Middle East](19323.md) and [North Africa](21714.md) after the expulsion of the Jews from Spain and Portugal in the 1490s and the pre-existing Jewish communities in those regions.[^cite_note-EJ571-278]

Ashkenazi Jews represent the bulk of modern Jewry, with at least 70 percent of Jews worldwide (and up to 90 percent prior to [World War II](32927.md) and [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust)). As a result of their [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) from [Europe](9239.md), Ashkenazim also represent the overwhelming majority of Jews in the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World) continents, in countries such as the [United States](3434750.md), [Canada](5042916.md), [Argentina](18951905.md), [Australia](4689264.md), [Brazil](3383.md), and [Uruguay](31843.md). In [France](5843419.md), the immigration of Jews from [Algeria](358.md) (Sephardim) has led them to outnumber the Ashkenazim.[^cite_note-EJ571-278] Only in [Israel](9282173.md) is the Jewish population representative of all groups, a [melting pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_pot) independent of each group's proportion within the overall world Jewish population.[^cite_note-279]

### Genetic studies

Main article: [Genetic studies on Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_studies_on_Jews)

[Y DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_chromosome) studies tend to imply a small number of founders in an old population whose members parted and followed different migration paths.[^cite_note-hammer2000-280] In most Jewish populations, these male line ancestors appear to have been mainly [Middle Eastern](19323.md). For example, [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews) share more common paternal lineages with other Jewish and Middle Eastern groups than with non-Jewish populations in areas where Jews lived in [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe), [Germany](11867.md), and the French [Rhine Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine). This is consistent with Jewish traditions in placing most Jewish paternal origins in the region of the Middle East.[^cite_note-Nebel_2001-281][^cite_note-282]

Conversely, the maternal lineages of Jewish populations, studied by looking at [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA), are generally more heterogeneous.[^cite_note-Behar2008b-283] Scholars such as [Harry Ostrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Ostrer) and Raphael Falk believe this indicates that many Jewish males found new mates from European and other communities in the places where they migrated in the diaspora after fleeing ancient Israel.[^cite_note-Lewontin-284] In contrast, Behar has found evidence that about 40 percent of Ashkenazi Jews originate maternally from just four female founders, who were of Middle Eastern origin. The populations of Sephardi and Mizrahi Jewish communities "showed no evidence for a narrow founder effect."[^cite_note-Behar2008b-283] Subsequent studies carried out by Feder et al. confirmed the large portion of non-local maternal origin among Ashkenazi Jews. Reflecting on their findings related to the maternal origin of Ashkenazi Jews, the authors conclude, "Clearly, the differences between Jews and non-Jews are far larger than those observed among the Jewish communities. Hence, differences between the Jewish communities can be overlooked when non-Jews are included in the comparisons."[^cite_note-Abraham_2010-16][^cite_note-285][^cite_note-Ostrer-2013-286] However, a 2025 genetic study on the Ashkenazi Jewish founder population supports the presence of a substantial Near Eastern component in the maternal lineages. Analyses of mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) indicate that the core founder lineages, estimated at around 54, likely originated from the Near East, with these founder signatures appearing in multiple copies across the population. While later admixture introduced additional mtDNA lineages, these absorbed lineages are distinguishable from the original founders. The findings are consistent with genome-wide Identity-by-Descent and Lineage Extinction analyses, reinforcing the Near Eastern origin of the Ashkenazi maternal founders.[^cite_note-Livni-2025-287] A study showed that 7% of Ashkenazi Jews have the haplogroup G2c, which is mainly found in [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns) and on lower scales all major Jewish groups, Palestinians, Syrians, and Lebanese.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289]

Studies of [autosomal DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autosome), which look at the entire DNA mixture, have become increasingly important as the technology develops. They show that Jewish populations have tended to form relatively closely related groups in independent communities, with most in a community sharing significant ancestry in common.[^cite_note-290] For Jewish populations of the diaspora, the genetic composition of [Ashkenazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews), [Sephardic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews), and [Mizrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews) Jewish populations show a predominant amount of shared Middle Eastern ancestry. According to Behar, the most parsimonious explanation for this shared Middle Eastern ancestry is that it is "consistent with the historical formulation of the Jewish people as descending from ancient [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrews) and [Israelite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) residents of the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant)" and "the dispersion of the people of ancient Israel throughout the [Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World)".[^cite_note-discovermagazine-291] [North African](21714.md), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Peninsula) and others of [Iberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) origin show variable frequencies of admixture with non-Jewish historical host populations among the maternal lines. In the case of Ashkenazi and Sephardi Jews (in particular [Moroccan Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moroccan_Jews)), who are closely related, the source of non-Jewish admixture is mainly [Southern European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe), while Mizrahi Jews show evidence of admixture with other Middle Eastern populations. The 2010 study by Avshalom Zoossmann-Diskin analyzed sex chromosomes and seventeen autosomal markers, determining that Eastern European Jews are closer genetically to modern [Italians](385155.md), and other [Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europeans) than to other Jewish populations.[^cite_note-discovermagazine-291][^cite_note-zooss-292] A 2001 study found that Jews were more closely related to groups of the Fertile Crescent (Kurds, Turks, and Armenians) than to their Arab neighbors, whose genetic signature was found in geographic patterns reflective of Islamic conquests.[^cite_note-Nebel_2001-281][^cite_note-293]

The studies also show that [Sephardic Bnei Anusim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Bnei_Anusim) (descendants of the "[anusim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anusim)" who were [forced to convert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_conversion) to [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism)), which comprise up to 19.8 percent of the population of today's [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberia) ([Spain](26667.md) and [Portugal](23033.md)) and at least 10 percent of the population of [Ibero-America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibero-America) ([Hispanic America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America) and [Brazil](3383.md)), have Sephardic Jewish ancestry within the last few centuries. The [Bene Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bene_Israel) and [Cochin Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochin_Jews) of [India](14533.md), [Beta Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_Israel) of [Ethiopia](187749.md), and a portion of the [Lemba people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lemba_people) of [Southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa), despite more closely resembling the local populations of their native countries, have also been thought to have some more remote ancient Jewish ancestry.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-discovermagazine-291][^cite_note-in.reuters.com-295][^cite_note-Ostrer-2013-286] Views on the Lemba have changed and genetic [Y-DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y-DNA) analyses in the 2000s have established a partially Middle-Eastern origin for a portion of the male Lemba population but have been unable to narrow this down further.[^cite_note-SpurdleJenkins-296][^cite_note-Soodyall-297]

### Population centers

For a more comprehensive list, see [Jewish population by city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population_by_city).



[New York City](645042.md) is home to 960,000 Jews, making it the [largest Jewish community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_New_York_City) outside of Israel.

Although historically, Jews have been found all over the world, in the decades since World War II and the establishment of Israel, they have increasingly concentrated in a small number of countries.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] In 2021, [Israel](9282173.md) and the [United States](3434750.md) together accounted for over 85 percent of the global Jewish population, with approximately 45.3% and 39.6% of the world's Jews, respectively.[^cite_note-JDB-3] More than half (51.2%) of world Jewry resides in just ten metropolitan areas. As of 2021, these ten areas were [Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gush_Dan), [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area), [Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Jerusalem), [Haifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haifa_metropolitan_area), [Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Los_Angeles), [Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_metropolitan_area), [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_metropolitan_area), [Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_metropolitan_area), [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area), and [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area). The Tel Aviv metro area has the highest percent of Jews among the total population (94.8%), followed by Jerusalem (72.3%), Haifa (73.1%), and Beersheba (60.4%), the balance mostly being Israeli Arabs. Outside Israel, the highest percent of Jews in a metropolitan area was in New York (10.8%), followed by Miami (8.7%), Philadelphia (6.8%), San Francisco (5.1%), Washington (4.7%), Los Angeles (4.7%), Toronto (4.5%), and Baltimore (4.1%).[^cite_note-JDB-3]

As of 2010, there were nearly 14 million Jews around the world, roughly 0.2% of the world's population at the time.[^cite_note-Pew-2015-300] According to the 2007 estimates of [The Jewish People Policy Planning Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jewish_People_Policy_Planning_Institute), the world's Jewish population is 13.2 million.[^cite_note-haaretz.com-301] This statistic incorporates both practicing Jews affiliated with [synagogues](48559.md) and the Jewish community, and approximately 4.5 million unaffiliated and [secular Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_secularism).

According to [Sergio Della Pergola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Della_Pergola), a demographer of the [Jewish population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population), in 2021 there were about 6.8 million Jews in Israel, 6 million in the United States, and 2.3 million in the rest of the world.[^cite_note-JDB-3]

#### Israel

Main article: [Israeli Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Jews)



Jewish people in [Jerusalem](16043.md)

[Israel](9282173.md), the Jewish nation-state, is the only country in which Jews make up a majority of the citizens.[^cite_note-302] Israel was established as an independent [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy) and Jewish state on 14 May 1948.[^cite_note-cia-303] Of the 120 members in its parliament, the [Knesset](74656.md),[^cite_note-304] as of 2016, 14 members of the Knesset are [Arab citizens of Israel](492331.md) (not including the Druze), most representing Arab political parties. One of Israel's [Supreme Court](1666510.md) judges is also an Arab citizen of Israel.[^cite_note-305]

Between 1948 and 1958, the Jewish population rose from 800,000 to two million.[^cite_note-306] Currently, Jews account for 75.4 percent of the Israeli population, or 6 million people.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] The early years of the State of Israel were marked by the [mass immigration](351513.md) of [Holocaust survivors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_survivors) in the [aftermath of the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_the_Holocaust) and Jews [fleeing Arab lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_exodus_from_Arab_and_Muslim_countries).[^cite_note-persecution-309] Israel also has a large population of [Ethiopian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Jews), many of whom were airlifted to Israel in the late 1980s and early 1990s.[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] Between 1974 and 1979 nearly 227,258 immigrants arrived in Israel, about half being from the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-312] This period also saw an increase in [immigration to Israel](351513.md) from [Western Europe](33800.md), [Latin America](18524.md), and [North America](21139.md).[^cite_note-313]

A trickle of immigrants from other communities has also arrived, including [Indian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Jews) and others, as well as some descendants of [Ashkenazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi) Holocaust survivors who had settled in countries such as the [United States](3434750.md), [Argentina](18951905.md), [Australia](4689264.md), [Chile](5489.md), [Uruguay](31843.md) and [South Africa](17416221.md).[^cite_note-314] Some Jews have emigrated from Israel elsewhere, because of economic problems or disillusionment with political conditions and the continuing [Arab–Israeli conflict](7960202.md). Jewish Israeli emigrants are known as [yordim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yerida).[^cite_note-Dosick_2007,_p._340-315]

#### Diaspora (outside Israel)

Main article: [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora)



In this [Rosh Hashana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosh_Hashana) greeting card from the early 1900s, Russian Jews, packs in hand, gaze at the American relatives beckoning them to the United States. Over two million Jews fled the [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogroms) of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) to the safety of the U.S. between 1881 and 1924.

[^cite_note-316]



A [menorah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menorah_\(Temple\)) dominating the main square in [Birobidzhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birobidzhan). An estimated 70,000 [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Russia_and_the_Soviet_Union) live in [Siberia](29639.md).

[^cite_note-317]

The waves of [immigration to the United States](15051.md) and elsewhere at the turn of the 19th century, the founding of [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) and later events, including [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogroms) in Imperial Russia (mostly within the [Pale of Settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pale_of_Settlement) in present-day Ukraine, Moldova, Belarus and eastern Poland), the massacre of European Jewry during [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust), and the founding of the [state of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Israel), with the subsequent [Jewish exodus from Arab lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_exodus_from_Arab_lands), all resulted in substantial shifts in the population centers of world Jewry by the end of the 20th century.[^cite_note-318]

More than half of the Jews live in the Diaspora (see Population table). Currently, the largest Jewish community outside Israel, and either the largest or second-largest Jewish community in the world, is located in the United States, with 6 million to 7.5 million Jews by various estimates. Elsewhere in the Americas, there are also large Jewish populations in [Canada](5042916.md) (315,000), [Argentina](18951905.md) (180,000–300,000), and [Brazil](3383.md) (196,000–600,000), and smaller populations in [Mexico](3966054.md), [Uruguay](31843.md), [Venezuela](32374.md), [Chile](5489.md), [Colombia](5222.md) and several other countries (see [History of the Jews in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Latin_America)).[^cite_note-JPPI2007-319] According to a 2010 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) study, about 470,000 people of Jewish heritage live in [Latin America](18524.md) and the [Caribbean](18956035.md).[^cite_note-Pew-2015-300] Demographers disagree on whether the United States has a larger Jewish population than Israel, with many maintaining that Israel surpassed the United States in Jewish population during the 2000s, while others maintain that the United States still has the largest Jewish population in the world. Currently, a major national Jewish population survey is planned to ascertain whether or not Israel has overtaken the United States in Jewish population.[^cite_note-320]



The Jewish [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) Youth Movement in [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn), Estonia, on 1 September 1933

[Western Europe](33800.md)'s largest Jewish community, and the third-largest Jewish community in the world, can be found in [France](5843419.md), home to between 483,000 and 500,000 Jews, the majority of whom are immigrants or refugees from North African countries such as [Algeria](358.md), [Morocco](19291.md), and [Tunisia](30188.md) (or their descendants).[^cite_note-321] The [United Kingdom](31717.md) has a Jewish community of 292,000. In [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Europe), the exact figures are difficult to establish. The number of Jews in Russia varies widely according to whether a source uses census data (which requires a person to choose a single nationality among choices that include "Russian" and "Jewish") or eligibility for immigration to Israel (which requires that a person have one or more Jewish grandparents). According to the latter criteria, the heads of the Russian Jewish community assert that up to 1.5 million Russians are eligible for [aliyah](351513.md).[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] In [Germany](11867.md), the 102,000 Jews registered with the Jewish community are a slowly declining population,[^cite_note-324] despite the immigration of tens of thousands of Jews from the former [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) since the fall of the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall).[^cite_note-325] Thousands of [Israelis](181296.md) also live in Germany, either permanently or temporarily, for economic reasons.[^cite_note-326]

Prior to 1948, approximately 800,000 Jews were living in lands which now make up the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) (excluding Israel). Of these, just under two-thirds lived in the French-controlled [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb) region, 15 to 20 percent in the [Kingdom of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Iraq), approximately 10 percent in the [Kingdom of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Egypt) and approximately 7 percent in the [Kingdom of Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yemen). A further 200,000 lived in [Pahlavi Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran) and the [Republic of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Turkey). Today, around 26,000 Jews live in Muslim-majority countries,[^cite_note-327] mainly in [Turkey](11125639.md) (14,200)[^cite_note-328] and [Iran](14653.md) (9,100), while [Morocco](19291.md) (2,000), [Tunisia](30188.md) (1,000), and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) (500) host the largest communities in the Arab world.[^cite_note-329] A small-scale exodus had begun in many countries in the early decades of the 20th century, although the only substantial [aliyah](351513.md) came from [Yemen](350939.md) and [Syria](7515849.md).[^cite_note-330] The [exodus from Arab and Muslim countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_exodus_from_Arab_and_Muslim_countries) took place primarily from 1948. The first large-scale exoduses took place in the late 1940s and early 1950s, primarily in [Iraq](7515928.md), Yemen and [Libya](17633.md), with up to 90 percent of these communities leaving within a few years. The peak of the exodus from [Egypt](8087628.md) occurred in 1956. The exodus in the Maghreb countries peaked in the 1960s. [Lebanon](17771.md) was the only Arab country to see a temporary increase in its Jewish population during this period, due to an influx of refugees from other Arab countries, although by the mid-1970s the Jewish community of Lebanon had also dwindled. In the aftermath of the exodus wave from Arab states, an additional migration of [Iranian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Jews) peaked in the 1980s when around 80 percent of Iranian Jews left the country.

Outside [Europe](9239.md), the [Americas](29833.md), the [Middle East](19323.md), and the rest of [Asia](689.md), there are significant Jewish populations in [Australia](4689264.md) (112,500) and [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population_of_South_Africa) (70,000).[^cite_note-JVIL2010-50] There is also a 6,800-strong community in [New Zealand](4913064.md).[^cite_note-331]

### Demographic changes

Main article: [Historical Jewish population comparisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Jewish_population_comparisons)

#### Assimilation

Main articles: [Jewish assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_assimilation) and [Interfaith marriage in Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage_in_Judaism)

Since at least the time of the [Ancient Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), a proportion of Jews have assimilated into the wider non-Jewish society around them, by either choice or force, ceasing to practice Judaism and losing their [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity).[^cite_note-Johnson171-332] Assimilation took place in all areas, and during all time periods,[^cite_note-Johnson171-332] with some Jewish communities, for example, the [Kaifeng Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaifeng_Jews) of [China](5405.md), disappearing entirely.[^cite_note-333] The advent of the Jewish Enlightenment of the 18th century (see [Haskalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskalah)) and the subsequent [emancipation of the Jewish populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_emancipation) of Europe and America in the 19th century, accelerated the situation, encouraging Jews to increasingly participate in, and become part of, [secular society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism). The result has been a growing trend of assimilation, as Jews marry non-Jewish spouses and stop participating in the Jewish community.[^cite_note-334]

Rates of [interreligious marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage) vary widely: In the United States, it is just under 50 percent;[^cite_note-335] in the United Kingdom, around 53 percent; in France, around 30 percent;[^cite_note-336] and in Australia and Mexico, as low as 10 percent.[^cite_note-337] In the United States, only about a third of children from intermarriages affiliate with Jewish religious practice.[^cite_note-338] The result is that most countries in the [Diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) have steady or slightly declining religiously Jewish populations as Jews continue to assimilate into the countries in which they live.

#### War and persecution

Further information: [Persecution of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews), [Antisemitism](1078.md), and [Jewish military history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_military_history)



The Roman Emperor [Nero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nero) sends [Vespasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vespasian) with an army to destroy the Jews, 69 CE.

The Jewish people and [Judaism](15624.md) have experienced various [persecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution) throughout [their history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_history). During [Late Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Antiquity) and the [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages), the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) (in its later phases known as the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire)) repeatedly repressed the [Jewish population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Roman_Empire), first by ejecting them from their homelands during the pagan [Roman era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_era) and later by officially establishing them as [second-class citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I#Suppression_of_religions) during the Christian Roman era.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340]

According to [James Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_P._Carroll), "Jews accounted for 10% of the total population of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire). By that ratio, if other factors had not intervened, there would be 200 million Jews in the world today, instead of something like 13 million."[^cite_note-341]

Later in [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) Western Europe, further persecutions of Jews by Christians occurred, notably during the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades)—when Jews all over Germany [were massacred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhineland_massacres)—and in a series of expulsions from the [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Expulsion), Germany, and France. Then there occurred the [largest expulsion of all](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhambra_Decree), when Spain and Portugal, after the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista) (the Catholic Reconquest of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula)), expelled both unbaptized Sephardic Jews and the ruling Muslim [Moors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moors).[^cite_note-Johnson207-208-342][^cite_note-Johnson213plus-343]

In the [Papal States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_States), which existed until 1870, Jews were required to live only in specified neighborhoods called [ghettos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto).[^cite_note-344]



[World War I](4764461.md) poster showing a soldier cutting the bonds from a Jewish man, who says, "You have cut my bonds and set me free—now let me help you set others free!"

[Islam and Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_Judaism) have a complex relationship. Traditionally Jews and Christians living in Muslim lands, known as [dhimmis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmis), were allowed to practice their religions and administer their internal affairs, but they were subject to certain conditions.[^cite_note-Bernard1020-345] They had to pay the [jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya) (a per capita tax imposed on free adult non-Muslim males) to the Islamic state.[^cite_note-Bernard1020-345] Dhimmis had an inferior status under Islamic rule. They had several social and legal [disabilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disabilities_\(Jewish\)) such as prohibitions against bearing arms or giving testimony in courts in cases involving Muslims.[^cite_note-346] Many of the disabilities were highly symbolic. The one described by [Bernard Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lewis) as "most degrading"[^cite_note-Lewis131-347] was the requirement of [distinctive clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_badge), not found in the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) or [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith) but invented in [early medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages) Baghdad; its enforcement was highly erratic.[^cite_note-Lewis131-347] On the other hand, Jews rarely faced martyrdom or exile, or forced compulsion to change their religion, and they were mostly free in their choice of residence and profession.[^cite_note-348]

Notable exceptions include the massacre of Jews and forcible conversion of some Jews by the rulers of the [Almohad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohad_Caliphate) dynasty in [Al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus) in the 12th century,[^cite_note-349] as well as in [Islamic Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_conquest_of_Persia),[^cite_note-350] and the forced confinement of Moroccan Jews to walled quarters known as [mellahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mellah) beginning from the 15th century and especially in the early 19th century.[^cite_note-351] In modern times, it has become commonplace for standard [antisemitic themes to be conflated with anti-Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionism_and_antisemitism) publications and pronouncements of Islamic movements such as [Hezbollah](13919.md) and [Hamas](13913.md), in the pronouncements of various agencies of the [Islamic Republic of Iran](14653.md), and even in the newspapers and other publications of Turkish [Refah Partisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refah_Partisi)."[^cite_note-Lewis_MEQ-352]

Throughout history, many rulers, empires and nations have oppressed their Jewish populations or sought to eliminate them entirely. Methods employed ranged from [expulsion](412990.md) to outright [genocide](12441.md); within nations, often the threat of these extreme methods was sufficient to silence dissent. The [history of antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_antisemitism) includes the [First Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Crusade) which resulted in the massacre of Jews;[^cite_note-Johnson207-208-342] the [Spanish Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition) (led by [Tomás de Torquemada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_de_Torquemada)) and the [Portuguese Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Inquisition), with their persecution and *[autos-da-fé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto-da-f%C3%A9)* against the [New Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Christians) and [Marrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marrano) Jews;[^cite_note-353] the [Bohdan Chmielnicki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohdan_Chmielnicki) Cossack massacres in [Ukraine](31750.md);[^cite_note-354] the [Pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom) backed by the Russian [Tsars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_rulers);[^cite_note-Johnson_1987,_pp._364–365-355] as well as expulsions from Spain, Portugal, England, France, Germany, and other countries in which the Jews had settled.[^cite_note-Johnson213plus-343] According to a 2008 study published in the *[American Journal of Human Genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Journal_of_Human_Genetics)*, 19.8 percent of the modern [Iberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) population has Sephardic Jewish ancestry,[^cite_note-Adams2008-356] indicating that the number of [conversos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Converso) may have been much higher than originally thought.[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358]



Jews in [Minsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk), 1941. Before World War II, some 40 percent of the population was Jewish. By the time the Red Army retook the city on 3 July 1944, there were only a few Jewish survivors.

The persecution reached a peak in [Nazi Germany](21212.md)'s [Final Solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Solution), which led to [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) and the slaughter of approximately 6 million Jews.[^cite_note-359] Of the world's 16 million Jews in 1939, almost 40% were murdered in the Holocaust.[^cite_note-360] The Holocaust—the state-led systematic [persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution) and [genocide](12441.md) of European Jews (and certain communities of North African Jews in [European controlled North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_North_Africa#European_colonization)) and other [minority groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_group) of Europe during [World War II](32927.md) by Germany and its [collaborators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collaboration_with_Nazi_Germany_and_Fascist_Italy)—remains the most notable modern-day persecution of Jews.[^cite_note-361] The persecution and [genocide](12441.md) were accomplished in stages. [Legislation to remove the Jews from civil society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_Laws) was enacted years before the outbreak of World War II.[^cite_note-362] [Concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_concentration_camps) were established in which inmates were used as [slave labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery) until they died of exhaustion or disease.[^cite_note-363] Where the [Third Reich](21212.md) conquered new territory in [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe), specialized units called [Einsatzgruppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einsatzgruppen) murdered Jews and political opponents in mass shootings.[^cite_note-BBC-Grave-364] Jews and [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) were crammed into [ghettos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghettos_in_Nazi-occupied_Europe) before being transported hundreds of kilometres by freight train to [extermination camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_camp) where, if they survived the journey, the majority of them were murdered in gas chambers.[^cite_note-365] Virtually every arm of Germany's bureaucracy was involved in the logistics of the mass murder, turning the country into what one Holocaust scholar has called "a genocidal nation."[^cite_note-Berenbaum103-366]

#### Migrations

Further information: [Expulsions of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsions_of_Jews)



Expulsions of Jews in Europe from 1100 to 1600

Throughout Jewish history, Jews have repeatedly been directly or indirectly expelled from both their original homeland, the [Land of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_of_Israel), and many of the areas in which they have settled. This experience as [refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_refugees) has shaped [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity) and religious practice in many ways, and is thus a major element of Jewish history.[^cite_note-367] In summary, the [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom) in Eastern Europe,[^cite_note-Johnson_1987,_pp._364–365-355] the rise of modern [antisemitism](1078.md),[^cite_note-Gartner_2001,_pp._213–5-368] the Holocaust,[^cite_note-369] as well as the rise of [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism),[^cite_note-370] all served to fuel the movements and migrations of huge segments of Jewry from land to land and continent to continent until they arrived back in large numbers at their original historical homeland in Israel.[^cite_note-Gartner431-371]

In the Hebrew Bible, the patriarch [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham) is described as a migrant to the land of [Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan) from [Ur of the Chaldees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ur_of_the_Chaldees).[^cite_note-372] His descendants, the [Children of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites), undertook [the Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Exodus) (meaning "departure" or "exit" in Greek) from [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), as described in the [Book of Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Exodus).[^cite_note-373]



Residents of [Lachish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Lachish), [Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah), being deported into exile following the [conquest of the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Lachish) by the [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire), c. 701 BCE

The first movement documented in the historical record occurred with the [resettlement policy of the Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resettlement_policy_of_the_Neo-Assyrian_Empire), which mandated the deportation of conquered peoples, and it is estimated some 4,500,000 among its captive populations suffered this dislocation over three centuries of Assyrian rule.[^cite_note-Smith-Christopher-374] With regard to Israel, [Tiglath-Pileser III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiglath-Pileser_III) claims he deported 80% of the population of [Lower Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Galilee), some 13,520 people.[^cite_note-375] Some 27,000 Israelites, 20 to 25% of the population of the [Kingdom of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(Samaria\)), were described as being deported by [Sargon II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargon_II), and were replaced by other deported populations and sent into permanent exile by Assyria, initially to the Upper Mesopotamian provinces of the Assyrian Empire.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] Between 10,000 and 80,000 people from the [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah) were similarly [exiled by Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_captivity),[^cite_note-Smith-Christopher-374] but these people were then returned to [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea) by [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) of the Persian [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire).[^cite_note-378]

Many Jews were exiled again by the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire).[^cite_note-379] The 2,000 year dispersion of the [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) beginning under the Roman Empire,[^cite_note-380] as Jews were spread throughout the Roman world and, driven from land to land,[^cite_note-381] settled wherever they could live freely enough to practice their religion. Over the course of the diaspora the center of Jewish life moved from [Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Iraq)[^cite_note-382] to the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_age_of_Jewish_culture_in_the_Iberian_Peninsula)[^cite_note-383] to [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Poland)[^cite_note-384] to the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_American)[^cite_note-385] and, as a result of [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism), back to [Israel](9282173.md).[^cite_note-Gartner431-371]



Etching of the [expulsion of the Jews from Frankfurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurter_Judengasse#The_Fettmilch_Uprising) in [1614](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_antisemitism). The text says: "1380 persons old and young were counted at the exit of the gate".

There were also many expulsions of Jews during the Middle Ages and the Enlightenment in Europe, including: 1290, 16,000 Jews were expelled from England (see the [Statute of Jewry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Jewry)); in 1396, 100,000 from France; in 1421, thousands were expelled from Austria. Many of these Jews settled in [East-Central Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East-Central_Europe), especially Poland.[^cite_note-Gartner_11-12-386] Following the [Spanish Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition) in 1492, the Spanish population of around 200,000 [Sephardic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi) Jews were expelled by the Spanish crown and [Catholic church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church), followed by expulsions in 1493 in Sicily (37,000 Jews) and Portugal in 1496. The expelled Jews fled mainly to the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), the Netherlands, and [North Africa](21714.md), others migrating to [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe) and the Middle East.[^cite_note-Johnson229-231-387]

&#x20;During the 19th century, France's policies of equal citizenship regardless of religion led to the immigration of Jews (especially from Eastern and Central Europe).[^cite_note-Johnson_1987,_p._306-388] This contributed to the arrival of millions of Jews in the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World). Over two million Eastern European Jews arrived in the United States from 1880 to 1925.[^cite_note-389]



Jews fleeing pogroms, 1882

In the latest phase of migrations, the [Islamic Revolution of Iran](347268.md) caused many [Iranian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Jews) to flee Iran. Most found refuge in the US (particularly [Los Angeles, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles,_California), and [Long Island, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Island,_New_York)) and Israel. Smaller communities of Persian Jews exist in Canada and Western Europe.[^cite_note-390] Similarly, when the [Soviet Union collapsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Soviet_Union_\(1985%E2%80%931991\)#Dissolution_of_the_USSR), many of the Jews in the affected territory (who had been [refuseniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refusenik)) were suddenly allowed to leave. This produced a wave of migration to Israel in the early 1990s.[^cite_note-Dosick_2007,_p._340-315]

#### Growth



Praying at the [Western Wall](75225.md)

Israel is the only country with a Jewish population that is consistently growing through [natural population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_population_growth), although the Jewish populations of other countries, in Europe and North America, have recently increased through immigration. In the Diaspora, in almost every country, the Jewish population in general is either declining or steady, but [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) and [Haredi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haredi) Jewish communities, whose members often shun [birth control](18978770.md) for religious reasons, have experienced rapid population growth.[^cite_note-391]

Orthodox and [Conservative Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism) discourage [proselytism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselytism) to non-Jews, but many Jewish groups have tried to reach out to the assimilated Jewish communities of the Diaspora for them to reconnect to their Jewish roots. Additionally, while in principle [Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism) favours seeking new members for the faith, this position has not translated into active proselytism, instead taking the form of an effort to reach out to non-Jewish spouses of intermarried couples.[^cite_note-392]

There is also a trend of Orthodox movements reaching out to secular Jews in order to give them a stronger [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity) so there is less chance of intermarriage. As a result of the efforts by these and other Jewish groups over the past 25 years, there has been a trend (known as the [Baal teshuva movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baal_teshuva_movement)) for secular Jews to become more religiously observant, though the demographic implications of the trend are unknown.[^cite_note-393] Additionally, there is also a growing rate of conversion to [Jews by Choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_by_Choice) of [gentiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentiles) who make the decision to head in the direction of becoming Jews.[^cite_note-394]

## Contributions

See also: [Lists of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_Jews)

Jewish individuals have played a significant role in the development and growth of [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture),[^cite_note-Cambridge_University_Historical_Series-64][^cite_note-britannica.com-65] advancing many fields of thought, [science and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture#Science_and_technology),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] both historically and in modern times,[^cite_note-395] including through discrete trends in [Jewish philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_philosophy), [Jewish ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethics)[^cite_note-Sekine-2005-58] and [Jewish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_literature),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] as well as specific trends in [Jewish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture), including in [Jewish art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_art), [Jewish music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_music), [Jewish humor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_humor), [Jewish theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_theatre), [Jewish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_cuisine) and [Jewish medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_medicine).[^cite_note-Rabin-60][^cite_note-Shatzmiller,_Joseph_1995-61] Jews have established various [Jewish political movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_political_movements),[^cite_note-Daly2013-56] [religious movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements), and, through the [authorship of the Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorship_of_the_Bible) and parts of the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament),[^cite_note-Dimont2004-396][^cite_note-Galambush2011-397] provided the foundation for [Christianity](5211.md) and [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-BarclaySweet1996-62][^cite_note-Paterson2009-63] More than 20 percent[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403] of the awarded [Nobel Prize](21201.md) have gone to individuals of [Jewish descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_Nobel_laureates).[^cite_note-404] Philanthropic giving is a widespread core function among Jewish organizations.[^cite_note-405]

"The death toll of the Great Revolt against the Roman empire amounted to about 600,000 Jews, whereas the Bar Kokhba revolt in 135 caused the death of about 500,000 Jews. Massacres account for roughly 40 percent of the decrease of the Jewish population in Palestine. Moreover, some Jews migrated to Babylon after these revolts because of the worse economic conditions. After accounting for massacres and migrations, there is an additional 30 to 40 percent of the decrease in the Jewish population in Palestine (about 1–1.3 million Jews) to be explained" (p. 19). [Boyarin, Daniel, and Jonathan Boyarin. 2003. Diaspora: Generation and the Ground of Jewish Diaspora. p. 714](http://nes.berkeley.edu/Web_Boyarin/BoyarinArticles/69%20Diaspora%20\(1993\).pdf) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201011234750/https://nes.berkeley.edu/Web_Boyarin/BoyarinArticles/69%20Diaspora%20\(1993\).pdf) 11 October 2020 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine) "...it is crucial to recognize that the Jewish conception of the Land of Israel is similar to the discourse of the Land of many (if not nearly all) "indigenous" peoples of the world. Somehow the Jews have managed to retain a sense of being rooted somewhere in the world through twenty centuries of exile from that someplace (organic metaphors are not out of place in this discourse, for they are used within the tradition itself). It is profoundly disturbing to hear Jewish attachment to the Land decried as regressive in the same discursive situations in which the attachment of native Americans or Australians to their particular rocks, trees, and deserts is celebrated as an organic connection to the Earth that "we" have lost" p. 714. [Cohen, Robin (1997), Global Diasporas: An Introduction. p. 24 London: UCL Press.](https://books.google.com/books?id=OcY0Oy5VpGIC\&q=greek\&pg=PA24) "...although the word Babylon often connotes captivity and oppression, a rereading of the Babylonian period of exile can thus be shown to demonstrate the development of a new creative energy in a challenging, pluralistic context outside the natal homeland. When the Romans destroyed the Second Temple in AD 70, it was Babylon that remained as the nerve- and brain-centre for Jewish life and thought...the crushing of the revolt of the Judaeans against the Romans and the destruction of the Second Temple by the Roman general Titus in AD 70 precisely confirmed the catastrophic tradition. Once again, Jews had been unable to sustain a national homeland and were scattered to the far corners of the world" (p. 24). [Johnson, Paul *A History of the Jews* "The Bar Kochba Revolt," (HarperPerennial, 1987) pp. 158–61](https://books.google.com/books?id=YBarWAR2qVkC\&pg=PA159): Paul Johnson analyzes Cassius Dio's *Roman History: Epitome of Book LXIX* para. 13–14 (Dio's passage cited separately) among other sources: "Even if Dio's figures are somewhat exaggerated, the casualties amongst the population and the destruction inflicted on the country would have been considerable. According to Jerome, many Jews were also sold into slavery, so many, indeed, that the price of Jewish slaves at the slave market in Hebron sank drastically to a level no greater than that for a horse. The economic structure of the country was largely destroyed. The entire spiritual and economic life of the Palestinian Jews moved to Galilee. Jerusalem was now turned into a Roman colony with the official name *Colonia Aelia Capitolina* (*Aelia* after Hadrian's family name: P. Aelius Hadrianus; *Capitolina* after Jupiter Capitolinus). The Jews were forbidden on pain of death to set foot in the new Roman city. Aelia thus became a completely pagan city, no doubt with the corresponding public buildings and temples... We can...be certain that a statue of Hadrian was erected in the centre of Aelia, and this was tantamount in itself to a desecration of Jewish Jerusalem." p. 159. [Cassius Dio's *Roman History: Epitome of Book LXIX* para. 13–14](https://penelope.uchicago.edu/Thayer/E/Roman/Texts/Cassius_Dio/69*.html): "13 At first the Romans took no account of them. Soon, however, all Judaea had been stirred up, and the Jews everywhere were showing signs of disturbance, were gathering together, and giving evidence of great hostility to the Romans, partly by secret and partly by overt acts; 2 many outside nations, too, were joining them through eagerness for gain, and the whole earth, one might almost say, was being stirred up over the matter. Then, indeed, Hadrian sent against them his best generals. First of these was Julius Severus, who was dispatched from Britain, where he was governor, against the Jews. 3 Severus did not venture to attack his opponents in the open at any one point, in view of their numbers and their desperation, but by intercepting small groups, thanks to the number of his soldiers and his under-officers, and by depriving them of food and shutting them up, he was able, rather slowly, to be sure, but with comparatively little danger, to crush, exhaust and exterminate them. Very few of them in fact survived. Fifty of their most important outposts and nine hundred and eighty-five of their most famous villages were razed to the ground. Five hundred and eighty thousand men were slain in the various raids and battles, and the number of those that perished by famine, disease and fire was past finding out. 2 Thus nearly the whole of Judaea was made desolate, a result of which the people had had forewarning before the war. For the tomb of Solomon, which the Jews regard as an object of veneration, fell to pieces of itself and collapsed, and many wolves and hyenas rushed howling into their cities. 3 Many Romans, moreover, perished in this war. Therefore Hadrian in writing to the senate did not employ the opening phrase commonly affected by the emperors, 'If you and our children are in health, it is well; I and the legions are in health'" (para. 13–14). Safran, William (2005). "The Jewish Diaspora in a Comparative and Theoretical Perspective". *Israel Studies*. **10** (1): 36–60. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.2979/ISR.2005.10.1.36](https://doi.org/10.2979%2FISR.2005.10.1.36). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [30245753](https://www.jstor.org/stable/30245753). [Project MUSE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Muse) [180371](https://muse.jhu.edu/article/180371). "...diaspora referred to a very specific case—that of the exile of the Jews from the Holy Land and their dispersal throughout several parts of the globe. Diaspora \[galut] connoted deracination, legal disabilities, oppression, and an often painful adjustment to a hostland whose hospitality was unreliable and ephemeral. It also connoted the existence on foreign soil of an expatriate community that considered its presence to be transitory. Meanwhile, it developed a set of institutions, social patterns, and ethnonational and/or religious symbols that held it together. These included the language, religion, values, social norms, and narratives of the homeland. Gradually, this community adjusted to the hostland environment and became itself a center of cultural creation. All the while, however, it continued to cultivate the idea of return to the homeland." (p. 36). Sheffer, Gabriel (2005). "Is the Jewish Diaspora Unique? Reflections on the Diaspora's Current Situation". *Israel Studies*. **10** (1): 1–35. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.2979/ISR.2005.10.1.1](https://doi.org/10.2979%2FISR.2005.10.1.1). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [30245752](https://www.jstor.org/stable/30245752). [Project MUSE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Muse) [180374](https://muse.jhu.edu/article/180374). "...the Jewish nation, which from its very earliest days believed and claimed that it was the "chosen people," and hence unique. This attitude has further been buttressed by the equally traditional view, which is held not only by the Jews themselves, about the exceptional historical age of this diaspora, its singular traumatic experiences its singular ability to survive pogroms, exiles, and Holocaust, as well as its "special relations" with its ancient homeland, culminating in 1948 with the nation-state that the Jewish nation has established there... First, like many other members of established diasporas, the vast majority of Jews no longer regard themselves as being in *Galut* \[exile] in their host countries....Perceptually, as well as actually, Jews permanently reside in host countries of their own free will, as a result of inertia, or as a result of problematic conditions prevailing in other hostlands, or in Israel. It means that the basic perception of many Jews about their existential situation in their hostlands has changed. Consequently, there is both a much greater self- and collective-legitimatization to refrain from making serious plans concerning "return" or actually "making Aliyah" \[to emigrate, or "go up"] to Israel. This is one of the results of their wider, yet still rather problematic and sometimes painful acceptance by the societies and political systems in their host countries. It means that they, and to an extent their hosts, do not regard Jewish life within the framework of diasporic formations in these hostlands as something that they should be ashamed of, hide from others, or alter by returning to the old homeland" (p. 4). Davies, William David; Finkelstein, Louis; Katz, Steven T. (1984). [*The Cambridge History of Judaism: Volume 4, The Late Roman-Rabbinic Period*](https://books.google.com/books?id=BjtWLZhhMoYC). Cambridge University Press. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-521-77248-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-521-77248-8). Although Dio's figure of 985 as the number of villages destroyed during the war seems hyperbolic, all Judaean villages, without exception, excavated thus far were razed following the Bar Kochba Revolt. This evidence supports the impression of total regional destruction following the war. Historical sources note the vast number of captives sold into slavery in Palestine and shipped abroad. ... The Judaean Jewish community never recovered from the Bar Kochba war. In its wake, Jews no longer formed the majority in Palestine, and the Jewish center moved to the Galilee. Jews were also subjected to a series of religious edicts promulgated by Hadrian that were designed to uproot the nationalistic elements with the Judaean Jewish community, these proclamations remained in effect until Hadrian's death in 138. An additional, more lasting punitive measure taken by the Romans involved expunging Judaea from the provincial name, changing it from Provincia Judaea to Provincia Syria Palestina. Although such name changes occurred elsewhere, never before or after was a nation's name expunged as the result of rebellion. Dalit Rom-Shiloni, *[Exclusive Inclusivity: Identity Conflicts Between the Exiles and the People who Remained (6th–5th Centuries BCE)](https://books.google.com/books?id=ffE3AAAAQBAJ\&pg=PR15)*, A\&C Black, 2013 p. xv n.3: 'it is argued that biblical texts of the Neo-Babylonian and the early Persian periods show a fierce adversarial relationship(s) between the Judean groups. We find no expressions of sympathy to the deported community for its dislocation, no empathic expressions towards the People Who Remained under Babylonian subjugation in Judah. The opposite is apparent: hostile, denigrating, and denunciating language characterizes the relationships between resident and exiled Judeans throughout the sixth and fifth centuries.' (p. xvii)

Despite the long regnant model that the Canaanites and Israelites were people of fundamentally different culture, archaeological data now casts doubt on this view. Until the modern period, the destruction of the Temple was the most cataclysmic moment in the history of the Jewish people. Without the Temple, the Sadducees no longer had any claim to authority, and they faded away. The sage Yochanan ben Zakkai, with permission from Rome, set up the outpost of Yavneh to continue develop of Pharisaic, or rabbinic, Judaism.

The vast bulk of Jews who dwelled abroad in the Second Temple Period did so voluntarily.' (2)' .Diaspora did not await the fall of Jerusalem to Roman power and destructiveness. [^cite_note-Ehrlich-2022-183]: &#x20;Ehrlich, Michael (2022). *The Islamization of the Holy Land, 634–1800*. Leeds, UK: Arc Humanities Press. pp. 3–4. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-64189-222-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-64189-222-3). [OCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OCLC_\(identifier\)) [1302180905](https://search.worldcat.org/oclc/1302180905). The Jewish community strove to recover from the catastrophic results of the Bar Kokhva revolt (132–135 CE). Although some of these attempts were relatively successful, the Jews never fully recovered. During the Late Roman and Byzantine periods, many Jews emigrated to thriving centres in the diaspora, especially Iraq, whereas some converted to Christianity and others continued to live in the Holy Land, especially in Galilee and the coastal plain. During the Byzantine period, the three provinces of Palestine included more than thirty cities, namely, settlements with a bishop see. After the Muslim conquest in the 630s, most of these cities declined and eventually disappeared. As a result, in many cases the local ecclesiastical administration weakened, while in others it simply ceased to exist. Consequently, many local Christians converted to Islam. Thus, almost twelve centuries later, when the army led by Napoleon Bonaparte arrived in the Holy Land, most of the local population was Muslim.

Although there are oases of Hebrew in certain schools, it has not become the Jewish lingua franca and English is rapidly taking its place as the Jewish people's language of communication. Even Hebrew-speaking Israeli representatives tend to use English in their public appearances at international Jewish conventions.

It is English rather than Hebrew that emerged as the *lingua franca* of the Jews towards the late 20th century.... This phenomenon occurred despite efforts to make Hebrew a language of communication, and despite the fact that the teaching of Hebrew was considered the *raison d'être* of the Jewish day schools and the 'nerve center' of Jewish learning.

Jews in [Tadzhikistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadzhikistan) have adopted [Tadzhik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajik_language) as their first language. The number of Yiddish-speaking Ashkenazic Jews in that region is comparatively low (cf. 2,905 in 1979). Both Ashkenazic and Oriental Jews have assimilated to Russian, the number of Jews speaking Russian as their first language amounting to a total of 6,564. It is reasonable to assume that the percentage of assimilated Ashkenazim is much higher than the portion of Oriental Jews.

A striking fact... is the high number of Laureates of the Jewish faith—over 20% of the total Nobel Prizes (138); including: 17% in Chemistry, 26% in Medicine and Physics, 40% in Economics and 11% in Peace and Literature each. The Jewish News of Northern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._The_Jewish_News_of_Northern_California)*. Retrieved 3 April 2012. Throughout the 20th century, Jews, more so than any other minority, ethnic or cultural group, have been recipients of the Nobel Prize—perhaps the most distinguished award for human endeavor in the six fields for which it is given. Remarkably, Jews constitute almost one-fifth of all Nobel laureates. This, in a world in which Jews number just a fraction of 1 percent of the population.

Similarly, because Jews make up less than a quarter of one percent of the world's population, it's surprising that over 20 percent of Nobel prizes have been awarded to Jews or people of Jewish descent.

[^cite_note-402]: &#x20;Lawrence E. Harrison (2008). *The Central Liberal Truth: How Politics Can Change a Culture and Save It*. [Oxford University Press](48518.md). p. 102. That achievement is symbolized by the fact that 15 to 20 percent of Nobel Prizes have been won by Jews, who represent two tenths of one percent of the world's population.

[^cite_note-403]: &#x20;Jonathan B. Krasner; Jonathan D. Sarna (2006). *The History of the Jewish People: Ancient Israel to 1880s America*. Behrman House, Inc. p. 1. These accomplishments account for 20 percent of the Nobel Prizes awarded since 1901. What a feat for a people who make up only .2 percent of the world's population!

[^cite_note-404]: &#x20;["Jewish Nobel Prize Winners"](http://www.jinfo.org/Nobel_Prizes.html). *Jinfo.org*. Retrieved 16 March 2016. At least 194 Jews and people of half- or three-quarters-Jewish ancestry have been awarded the Nobel Prize, accounting for 22% of all individual recipients worldwide between 1901 and 2015, and constituting 36% of all US recipients during the same period. In the scientific research fields of Chemistry, Economics, Physics, and Physiology/Medicine, the corresponding world and US percentages are 26% and 38%, respectively. Among women laureates in the four research fields, the Jewish percentages (world and US) are 33% and 50%, respectively. Of organizations awarded the Nobel Peace Prize, 22% were founded principally by Jews or by people of half-Jewish descent. Since the turn of the century (i.e., since the year 2000), Jews have been awarded 25% of all Nobel Prizes and 28% of those in the scientific research fields.
